Champions League, stasera Feyenoord-Inter: ecco tutte le curiosità sulla partita del de Kuip

Si avvicina una nuova tappa del cammino europeo dell'Inter: i nerazzurri saranno impegnati a Rotterdam contro il Feyenoord martedì 3 novembre alle ore 21:00. La sfida tra la squadra di Chivu e quella di van Bronckhorst, valida per la quarta giornata di League Phase della Champions League 2026/27, vedrà affrontarsi due squadre appaiate nella classifica generale a quota 3 punti dopo tre partite giocate. L'Inter, dopo le due sconfitte contro Real Madrid e Club Brugge, ha battuto lo Shakhtar a San Siro grazie alla rete di Carlos Augusto, mentre gli olandesi hanno perso nelle due trasferte disputate a Barcellona (5-1) e a Siviglia contro il Betis (1-0), trovando la vittoria al "De Kuip" contro il Como, sconfitto 3-2.

Una sfida che in Champions League è già andata in scena nella fase a eliminazione diretta della stagione 2024/25, con i nerazzurri che hanno avuto la meglio centrando la qualificazione ai quarti, e che ha vissuto incroci anche in Coppa Uefa e in amichevole. Quello contro il Feyenoord sarà il 23° confronto dell'Inter contro una squadra olandese in competizioni ufficiali: il bilancio conta 12 vittorie nerazzurre, 5 pareggi e 5 sconfitte. In attesa della sfida del "De Kuip", ecco precedenti, curiosità e intrecci tra Inter e Feyenoord.

Sette vittorie e quattro pareggi in undici giornate di campionato, primo posto in classifica a quota 25 punti, con 33 gol segnati e 12 subiti. Il Feyenoord ha iniziato la stagione in modo decisamente positivo in Eredivisie, ma è reduce da due pareggi nelle ultime due uscite in campionato: dopo l'1-1 in casa del PSV Eindhoven è arrivato quello interno contro il Fortuna Sittard, con il gol del pari ospite arrivato a cinque minuti dalla fine. Diverso il rendimento in Champions League, dove gli uomini di Van Bronckhorst hanno raccolto tre punti in tre partite, frutto dei k.o. esterni con Barcellona e Betis e il successo casalingo contro il Como. Nel match contro la squadra di Fabregas, giocato davanti al proprio pubblico, gli olandesi sono stati trascinati dalle giocate di Moussa e di Zechiel. Il primo, ala destra veloce e abile nell'uno contro uno, ama convergere sul sinistro e cercare la conclusione: proprio con un'azione del genere ha innescato il terzo gol del Feyenoord, con il suo mancino deviato che è entrato in porta dopo l'ultimo tocco di Sanchez. Il secondo, invece, è un centrocampista dinamico dagli ottimi tempi di inserimento, qualità nello stretto e capacità balistica: abilità che ha messo in mostra in occasione della rete del pari contro il Como, girandosi in un fazzoletto e concludendo dal limite dell'area.

Hill-Climbing Agent Quality with Agents CLI Evaluation

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A live walkthrough of the Agents CLI eval loop, from a first run to regression tracking, model-backend experiments, and automated alerting

Building an agent that works on a demo is a different problem from building an agent that stays working across model upgrades, tool changes, and new conversation shapes. The first takes a weekend; the second takes a loop. A team writes test cases, runs them, grades them, reads what failed, changes the agent, and runs the cases again. If the score goes up, the change was good. If it goes down, revert. Over weeks of this, the agent inches toward the ceiling of what the model, tools, and prompt can do together. The quality-engineering name for this is hill-climbing.

The Agents CLI eval namespace was built around this loop. The commands are small and framework-agnostic: eval generate runs the agent against a dataset of cases, eval grade scores the generated traces, eval compare diffs two run results side by side, eval analyze clusters failures into themes, and eval optimize runs an automated hill-climbing pass on the agent's prompt.

Below: baseline → regression → model swap → scheduling and alerting.

The scaffold used throughout is the LangChain template that ships with Agents CLI v1.8.0. The setup is a single command:

The scaffold ships with an eval dataset at tests/eval/datasets/basic-dataset.json, a config at tests/eval/eval_config.yaml, and a custom LLM-as-judge metric at tests/eval/response_quality.py. The config declares the metric to run:

The metric grades each response on a 1-to-5 scale for accuracy, relevance, and clarity, using gemini-3.8-flash as the judge. The default dataset has a single trivial case, which is not enough for a hill-climb. The first thing we do is expand it to four cases that probe different behaviors:

Four cases: a greeting, a weather question the mock tool handles with the hardcoded foggy response, a weather question for a city the tool defaults on, and an off-topic question that the agent has to decide how to handle. This is a small dataset by design; the hill-climbing loop pays off when the dataset is small enough to run on every change.

With the dataset in place, we run eval generate to drive the agent against each case, then eval grade to score the resulting traces:

Mean 4.0 out of 5 across four cases, with a standard deviation of 1.41. That stdev tells us immediately that the cases are not scoring uniformly, which is a healthier starting point than a flat 5.0 across the board. A flat 5.0 either means the agent is perfect (unlikely) or the dataset is too easy to show variation. 4.0 with real spread is a hill to climb.

To demonstrate the loop, we introduce a realistic regression. A developer working across several agent projects accidentally pastes the wrong system prompt into our weather agent: a chef persona meant for a different codebase. In app/agent.py:

The change compiles, the agent still runs, and nothing in the pre-eval pipeline (lint, unit tests, type checks) catches it. Re-running the same eval generate plus eval grade sequence produces:

Mean 2.75, down from 4.0. The judge agrees with our intuition that a chef persona on a weather agent is a worse product than a general-purpose helpful assistant. The stdev also tightened from 1.41 to 0.96, consistent with the chef prompt pushing most cases toward the same mediocre range.

A 1.25-point drop on a 1-to-5 scale is substantial, and the kind of regression that can slip through code review when the diff looks small ("one string literal changed"). The loop surfaced the change at all, with a specific number attached to it, before the agent reached production.

Mean scores are a start; the per-case diff is where the hill-climb happens. eval compare reads two result files and produces a structured JSON diff:

The output lists which keys changed and which stayed the same:

The differences object has the full per-case breakdown with the baseline and candidate values side by side. For a hill-climbing workflow, we typically pipe the output through jq or a small Python script to extract the signal; the raw JSON is the complete evidence.

Walking the two result files and lining up the scores per case shows where the regression landed:

Three cases dropped, one held. The most important of the three is sf_weather: the chef persona pulled the model's attention away from the registered get_weather tool, so the agent stopped calling it and started making up weather values. Unit tests can't catch this: the code runs, the tool is still registered, but the model's behavior around it has shifted. Only an end-to-end eval that scores the response in context surfaces this.

The mean drop by itself (−1.25) is a clear signal, but the per-case breakdown is what tells the developer where to look. A one-line system-prompt change propagated through three different cases in three different ways: greeting lost a point on tone, sf_weather lost two on tool usage, off_topic lost two on scope creep.

A common hill-climbing question is whether a cheaper or faster model would do the job acceptably. For the LangChain template, the model is defined in app/agent.py:

We swap gemini-3.8-flash for gemini-2.5-flash (restoring the helpful system prompt first), re-run eval generate plus eval grade, and compare the two result files. The mean drops from 4.00 to 2.75, the same magnitude as the chef-prompt regression, but the per-case breakdown tells a completely different story:

The cheaper model matched 3.8-flash exactly on the greeting and rome_weather cases and lost a point on sf_weather (slightly less polished phrasing). The whole visible drop traces to off_topic, where the cheaper model refused with a hedge about the event "not having happened yet" (a training-cutoff artifact), and the judge marked it harshly.

The lesson is that the summary number is misleading. Look only at the mean and you'd call gemini-2.5-flash as bad as the chef prompt. Look at the per-case breakdown and you'd call it a reasonable swap for most of the agent's workload, with one case (handling out-of-scope questions about recent events) exposing a real limitation. If the production distribution of user questions matches the first three cases more than the fourth, the swap is worth considering. If off_topic-style questions are common, it is not.

Two caveats on this experiment. First, model swaps interact with the prompt: a prompt tuned for a stronger model often leaks capability when ported to a weaker one, so the right comparison is "weaker model with a prompt tuned for it" versus "stronger model with its own tuned prompt." Second, the Vertex AI Eval Service calls a judge model to grade the traces, and the judge is held constant across the comparison. Changing the judge is a different experiment and should be done separately.

Agents CLI does not ship a scheduler, but the eval commands are designed to drop into standard infrastructure. The pattern we can use here is a cron job or Cloud Scheduler trigger that runs eval generate plus eval grade on a cadence and writes the results to a persistent location:

A nightly run against a stable regression dataset catches drift from model updates, data changes in the agent's tools, and dependencies that shift under the agent's feet. For a production agent, the scheduled run also captures the model vendor's quiet upgrades, which can move the agent's behavior without any change on our side. The dataset for the scheduled run is typically larger than the one used in the inner loop (dozens to hundreds of cases rather than a few), since it only runs once a day.

The scheduled run produces a result file every day, but a file nobody looks at is not an alert. The pattern here can be a wrapper script that compares each new run's mean against a baseline (the previous run, or a committed known-good) and exits non-zero when the drop exceeds a threshold. The non-zero exit is what triggers the alert from the surrounding scheduler.

Running it against our regression demo (baseline 4.0, candidate 2.75 from the chef-prompt run):

And against an identical pair (self-compare):

The non-zero exit code is what a cron job, Cloud Scheduler, or GitHub Actions step picks up to send the alert.

For ADK projects, eval optimize runs an automated hill-climbing pass on the agent's prompt using the GEPA framework (not currently supported for the LangChain extension). The command takes a dataset and a target metric, proposes prompt variants, grades each one, keeps the variants that improve the metric, and iterates. The output is a set of candidate prompts ranked by their eval score, which a human then reviews and promotes to the agent definition.

Two things to understand about eval optimize. First, it is scoped to prompt optimization, not to arbitrary agent changes: the agent's tools, structure, and model stay fixed. Second, GEPA sometimes finds genuine improvements and sometimes finds prompt variants that score higher on the specific metric by overfitting to its wording. The reviewer is the control. For projects where the loop applies, the discipline is to run eval optimize on the regression dataset (where improvements are measurable) and to review the candidate prompts carefully before promotion.

A few patterns and anti-patterns from running this loop ourselves and watching teams do the same.

Lead with a small, representative dataset, not a comprehensive one. The temptation when starting an eval is to try to cover every edge case the agent will ever see. The result is a dataset that takes an hour to run and that nobody runs on a change. Four cases that run in under a minute, as above, exercise the main behaviors and get run on every change. A regression dataset of three hundred cases that runs for an hour becomes a release gate at best and shelfware at worst.

Snapshot result files, not scores. The score is a summary; the result file is the evidence. When a regression surfaces three weeks after a prompt change, having the full result JSON from before and after the change (not just the mean score) is what lets eval compare show the specific case that moved. The per-case breakdown in Step 4 above would not have been possible from scores alone.

Separate the dataset from its expectations. The dataset is the inputs. The eval config is the expectations. They evolve on different cadences: the dataset grows as new real-world cases surface in production, and the config evolves as the team's definition of "good" matures. Keeping them in separate files, both committed to version control, makes it clear which half changed when a score moves.

Treat summary metrics with suspicion. The model-swap experiment in Step 5 is the clearest example. The mean dropped by half a point, but the per-case breakdown showed the drop was concentrated in one case while the others held or improved. A team that only looked at the mean would have made the wrong call.

More evals do not automatically lead to more fixes. A recent controlled study we ran found that coding agents using agents-cli run four times as many evals as coding agents without it, but change the agent after an eval about as often as the baseline. The behavior gain comes from what the skills put into the build at the start, not from the inner iteration. Evals are necessary for quality, but the quality comes from acting on what the evals show.

Scaffold an Agents CLI project and run the first eval loop in a handful of commands:

The full eval namespace is documented at google.github.io/agents-cli/cli. The source code, including the first-party eval primitives, is at github.com/google/agents-cli.

Smaller apps and faster GPU inference with LiteRT in Google Play services

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

LiteRT in Google Play services is Android's on-device inference runtime for custom models, handling more than a trillion inferences a day across billions of devices. Today, it gets its biggest upgrade yet: GPU inference that runs about 1.4x faster than the legacy GPU delegate, powered by ML Drift and available through the new CompiledModel API, LiteRT’s modern successor to the legacy Interpreter API.

Until now, taking advantage of this faster GPU stack required bundling LiteRT in your app. In our testing, apps that switched to LiteRT in Google Play services are about 8 MB smaller, making them faster to download and lighter on users' devices, because the runtime, including the GPU accelerator, ships once with Play services rather than inside every app. More than 100K Android apps bundle their own copy of LiteRT or TensorFlow Lite today, so the savings add up quickly as apps switch.

If your Android app runs custom ML models on CPU or GPU, we encourage you to switch to LiteRT in Google Play services. You can see it in action in the Google AI Edge Gallery app, where the Scrapbook feature's image segmentation runs on LiteRT in Google Play services.

Developers that adopt LiteRT in Google Play Services enjoy immediate benefits:

LiteRT is the evolution of TensorFlow Lite. It keeps the same .tflite model format you rely on and adds the CompiledModel API, which makes acceleration across fragmented hardware reliable:

Whether you're starting fresh or switching from bundled LiteRT, using LiteRT in Google Play services takes a few lines of code:

If you're already using the legacy Interpreter API in Google Play services, your Interpreter code keeps working on CPU when you switch to play-services-litert. To run on the GPU, move to the CompiledModel API.

Let your coding agent do the work. In Agent Mode, the new LiteRT skill for Android Studio automates the move to LiteRT in Google Play services and the CompiledModel API. Ask it to “migrate my TFLite code to CompiledModel,” or type @litert-runtime, and it switches your app to play-services-litert, rewrites your Interpreter calls, and sets up GPU acceleration with CPU fallback. It can also scaffold a new LiteRT app. Treat it as a strong first pass: review the diff, build, and compare outputs on a real device. Get the skill on GitHub.

Upgrading from legacy APIs? The legacy GPU delegate and custom delegate APIs are deprecated in favor of CompiledModel GPU acceleration and the Compiler Plugin and Dispatch APIs, and NNAPI will be removed starting in 2027. Older SDKs now receive critical security fixes only. Apps you've already shipped keep working, but plan your move to the CompiledModel API now: code that uses deprecated APIs won't compile against the latest SDK, and staying on an older SDK can cause conflicts as your other dependencies move forward. See the release notes for details.

Looking ahead, LiteRT in Google Play services is our foundation for running your own task-specific models on Android, with CPU and GPU acceleration.

Build Generative UI Apps with A2UI & CopilotKit Agents

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Agent-User Interactions are now evolving from text exchanges to rich UIs. Until recently, most conversations looked like this:

User: "Find me a holiday package to Bali in March." Agent: (six packages in prose, each with dates, hotel, flights, price) User: "The five-night one with breakfast." Agent: "Here's the link. You can book it on the operator's site."

Agents could plan the trip, but everything came back as walls of text. They had no standard way to render a selectable list of packages, a map of the destinations, or a date picker to carry you through the rest of the booking, inside the conversation itself.

Generative UI is the layer that lets agents build interfaces at runtime, shaped by what each request needs - giving users a rich, interactive experience. Implementing that takes two pieces: a way to describe the UI and an agentic stack that connects your agent to your application and renders that UI.

Generative UI covers a spectrum of approaches, from using predefined higher order components, to enabling your AI agent to have the flexibility to decide how to build the UI.

At the controlled end, you build each component ahead of time, and the agent picks which one to render and with what data. It's brand-safe but it does not allow for deep customization and personalization based on the user’s intent. At the open end, the agent returns a raw UI program written in HTML, Javascript and/or Dart that renders inside a sandboxed iframe. That covers anything you did not plan for, at the cost of a surface that drifts from your design system and executes code a model wrote.

A2UI works through the entire spectrum. It participates in controlled generation with decision models like Typesafe’s Jev and also open-ended code generation that may output A2UI JSON messages. The declarative mode, where agents assemble pre-declared components and functions, is the most popular. A2UI is the framework-agnostic standard for declaring UI intent, so local or remote agents can generate your UI from your existing component catalog using a common language.

Frontend developers can now ship the building blocks once, and the agent composes them for each request.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The Basic Catalog ships 18 general-purpose primitives across layout, display, input, and containers. The agent can compose entirely different interfaces from the same 18 primitives, depending on what the request needs.

The Basic Catalog is deliberately sparse, so most production apps define their own custom catalog. A custom component can be anything your frontend already renders, including a live map the agent fills with coordinates while your renderer draws the route.

CopilotKit is built on AG-UI (Agent-User Interaction Protocol), which supports A2UI payload and provides Agent SDKs across many agentic backends.

Your agent emits A2UI operations. AG-UI carries them to the client as typed events. When the user takes any action, the values are sent back along the same stream.

A2UI works with any AG-UI compatible agent. Frameworks like Google ADK, LangChain, Mastra and Strands are all AG-UI compatible, so the setup is identical whichever one your agent runs on.

Setup comes down to two things: connecting your agent to CopilotKit, and defining which components the agent is allowed to use.

Using Google ADK as the example, the ag_ui_adk package wraps any ADK agent and serves it as an AG-UI endpoint on FastAPI:

On the app side, you register that endpoint with the CopilotKit runtime at /api/copilotkit. It's a few lines of standard setup, covered in the Google ADK quickstart, and it needs nothing A2UI-specific.

A2UI itself turns on from the client, where your components already are. Define what the agent is allowed to draw, map each definition to a renderer, and pass the catalog to the CopilotKit provider.

The description goes into the agent's prompt, so the model knows when to reach for FlightCard instead of a generic card. The Zod schema validates the props it sends back.

The A2UI configuration can be set up ahead of time on the server, or it can be created dynamically, shipped from the client to the server with the request. The ADK code above used dynamic configuration which is why it used generate_a2ui without defining it. The docs cover both approaches.

That’s the CopilotKit path. You can also consume the event stream directly with @ag-ui/client and hand the A2UI operations to any compliant renderer: @a2ui/react, or the Flutter, Lit, or Angular equivalent.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Most agent-generated UI is one-shot. The agent draws something, you read it, and it goes stale the moment anything changes.

A2UI surfaces are bi-directional and the components are reactive, which makes multi-step flows possible. Any action the user takes on the interface (picking dates, choosing a flight, entering passenger details) travels back along the same stream, and the agent re-emits the surface from there using those values.

Here is a high-level architecture example of an end-to-end booking flow, with an AG-UI agent on Cloud Run streaming A2UI surfaces to a CopilotKit frontend.

Two things make the round trip work: inputs bound to a data path, and a button carrying an action that names which paths to send back.

Validation runs on the client, so a bad email shows an error inline without a model call. Nothing reaches the agent until the button fires.

Then the client sends the bound values, and the agent re-emits against the same surfaceId. That is how the whole flow lives on one surface: flight search, results, passenger details, and confirmation.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Every AG-UI integration inherets these benefits, so you don't have to build them yourself:

Create a complete A2UI + CopilotKit application. This creates a starter A2UI application with Copilotkit Agent integration.

The CopilotKit Dojo has runnable A2UI demos: fixed server-side schema, dynamic schema against a custom catalog, and an error-recovery example where an invalid first render heals into a valid one.

Explore how to build Generative UI Agents with CopilotKit, or dive into A2UI documentation to learn how to integrate A2UI with any framework of your choice.

DevFest is back!

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Technology is evolving at a breakneck speed—from generative AI and autonomous agents, to scalable cloud infrastructure and modern web standards. What better way to stay connected, than to roll up your sleeves, experiment, and build alongside a thriving global community at your local DevFest.

Build, Secure and Scale in the Agentic Era

DevFest is the world’s largest, annual, community-led event series aimed to educate developers and builders about Google’s latest technologies. Hosted by Google Developer Groups (GDG), DevFest will run from October 1 through December 31, 2026. Across 800+ global events, almost 1 million attendees will gather across 115 countries, to connect and learn how to build, secure, and scale in the agentic era.

Through interactive sessions and workshops, Devfest will deep dive into the latest Google AI technologies and also offers comprehensive tracks for Google Cloud, Android and Web, giving every developer and builder actionable tools to deploy real-world solutions.

Powered by Local Communities, Accessible to All

DevFest is uniquely built by the community, for the community. Hosted in local languages by grassroots organizers, each event is tailored to its regional ecosystem and features a diverse lineup of speakers, including Google Developer Experts (GDEs), Google engineers, and local innovators.

At its heart, DevFest is about community-driven problem-solving. These gatherings showcase that when developers connect, the impact extends far beyond the conference walls.

DevFest welcomes all learning levels, whether you are an experienced software engineer, an open-source contributor, founder, or curious beginner. Attend your local DevFest and start learning.

CrowdStrike to shell out nearly $500M for 2 AI security startups as the market for securing agents takes off

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Cybersecurity giant CrowdStrike is buying two AI security companies that expand its products to more broadly monitor, test, and cover AI models and agents, as companies across industries start rolling out AI agents and models wholesale.

CrowdStrike said it will buy Fiddler AI, which makes observability software for monitoring AI agents inside enterprises, and Dreadnode, which helps companies automate security testing specifically aimed at detecting flaws in AI models, apps and agents before they are set live.

The company is paying around $400 million for Fiddler AI, and about $95 million for Dreadnode, according to The Wall Street Journal.

The deals are happening at a time of significant upheaval in the cybersecurity space, especially as companies’ policies and processes lag behind AI adoption within their walls. Teams and individual employees alike are experimenting with AI tools, automating workflows, vibe-coding custom apps, and deploying armies of AI agents to do their bidding, which is understandably causing headaches for security teams.

CrowdStrike, whose products are used widely across enterprises and governments, is clearly trying to leverage its already significant heft and presence to capture a share of this quickly burgeoning market. It’s not alone, of course, as we’ve already seen several startups and cybersecurity vendors raising dozens of millions in funding to solve issues for companies, though most of these approaches are essentially based on the conviction that existing security software isn’t enough to monitor and control AI tools, connectors, or usage.

The company has itself been building detection and response controls, dubbed Falcon Guardian, to help companies see and understand what agents are doing on their devices, and these acquisitions seem to further flesh out its offerings.

Fiddler AI is going to be part of CrowdStrike’s Falcon platform as Falcon Observe, where it’ll ostensibly help the company’s clients evaluate agents before they’re deployed, understand their actions, and monitor what they do, and feed that information as telemetry into the broader platform. Dreadnode, meanwhile, will be integrated into the Falcon Guardian product, to which the startup’s software will feed its testing results as security policy recommendations.

Founded in 2018, Fiddler AI has raised a total of about $99 million to date, according to PitchBook. Dreadnode, founded in 2023, has raised $14 million so far.

When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. This doesn’t affect our editorial independence.

Ram is a financial and tech reporter and editor. He covered North American and European M&A, equity, regulatory news and debt markets at Reuters and Acuris Global, and has also written about travel, tourism, entertainment and books.

You can contact or verify outreach from Ram by emailing [email protected].

The Elder Scroll 6: Sentinel's Name Quietly Teases Juicy 'Skyrim' Sequel

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A closer look at how TES6 might connect to Skyrim and The Elder Scrolls Online

Eight years after the original announcement, we've finally learned the name of Bethesda's next RPG: The Elder Scrolls 6: Sentinel. Since the developer didn't show any gameplay footage, all fans can really do is muse on the meaning of the title. As disappointing as that is, Elder Scrolls loreheads do have plenty to chew on.

The subtitle has been an open secret for a while now — fans guessed it correctly years ahead of the reveal, and Xbox was teasing an eight-letter subtitle recently as well. The first and primary reason for this title should be obvious: it sounds cool. That's not the only reason, of course. Accompanying the title in the trailer was a short quote from Yazar, a Moth Priest serving High Queen Iradne of Hammerfell:

The scroll offered only a shimmer, a veil thinly lifted to my eyes. A future lost. A victory undone. Sands of time scattered by fate.

The Hammerfell setting has been rumored for a while now as well, but the quote cements it in stone. Hammerfell is a region to the north of Skyrim, and it is the home of the Redguard race. Redguards are known for their strength, combat prowess, and resilience in harsh climates. Accordingly, Hammerfell and its deserts, jungles, and mountain ranges were once known as the Deathlands.

Hammerfell will be immediately recognizable to anyone who has played The Elder Scrolls Online. And the most consequential region in Hammerfell is arguably — drumroll please — Sentinel. Sentinel’s proximity to the sea makes it a critical trading post for Tamriel at large. Five major areas of Sentinel are already explorable in ESO: the docks, a Redguard gym, a grand royal palace, a massive open-air marketplace, and a sewer system.

Moth priests are a part of a cult that devotes itself to reading the actual Elder Scrolls. These priests use the scrolls to foretell the future in an effort to help monarchs like the Emperor or, in this case, the regional queen. The timing of the quote — 4E 206 — places Sentinel five years after the events of Skyrim.

It's worth knowing that Hammerfell has spent centuries embroiled between its northern and southern regions, but the area was unified by the time Skyrim takes place. That unification was primarily meant to help save Sentinel from an opportunistic Elven incursion that tried to take advantage of the ongoing larger turmoil.

A five-year time jump would likely further Hammerfell's era as a sovereign state — but that doesn't necessarily signal stability. Hammerfell's independence separates it from the larger empire that could provide it support during a time of need. That's important, because the elves probably aren't done with Hammerfell, either.

There are two primary events in Skyrim that are of note here. One involves the Dark Brotherhood quest where the player assassinates Emperor Titus Mede II. A death like that opens the door for the elves to launch an assault while the wider empire tries to pick up the pieces after the assassination.

The other quest, which involves a Redguard noble person named Saadia, is a perfect encapsulation of what players can expect in The Elder Scrolls 6. In it, Saadia claims she is being hunted by elves for speaking out. After the player investigates further, it turns out that Saadia actually betrayed the Redguard to help the elves. This sets a precedent that the elves have already infiltrated the highest echelons of power in Sentinel — and will probably find more ways to sow discord in the new game.

The quote is ambiguous but could be read as an attempt by Hammerfell's High Queen to figure out how to proceed in what is undoubtedly a messy situation.

Hopefully, Bethesda won't keep fans guessing for too long. It's been nearly a decade since the game was originally announced, and the RPG has apparently been playable since 2025. Fans are ready for something more than just a title screen.

Tesla cuts FSD’s speed margin from 50% to 10% over the limit in Europe

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Tesla’s driver-assistance system in Europe can no longer be set to drive 50% over the speed limit. The Netherlands’ RDW, which approved the system in April, confirmed today that it signed off on an update capping the speed margin at 10% and officially renaming “FSD Supervised” to Tesla Assisted Driving.

The changes land weeks before EU member states could vote on approving the system across the bloc, and they match the conditions Germany set last month.

The RDW’s notice is the clearest official confirmation of how the system was configured when the Dutch regulator approved FSD Supervised on April 10. The “speed margin,” which lets the system deviate from the detected limit for “safety and traffic flow,” could go up to +50%. On a 100 km/h highway, that’s 150 km/h. In a 30 km/h city zone, it’s 45 km/h.

Now the margin tops out at +10%. It also resets to 0% every time the car starts, and the RDW says other speed-related functions are now disabled by default. It didn’t say which ones.

The new name shows up on the in-car display, in the owner’s manual, and in other driver information on HW4 vehicles. Tesla pushes all of it over the air.

The RDW describes the update as “several minor changes” that Tesla asked it to assess. The regulator says it checked the implementation, found no adverse or unintended effects, and folded the changes into the existing type approval without amending or extending it. It also stresses that the rename doesn’t change what the system does. It’s still driver assistance, and the driver is still responsible.

The RDW notified the nine member states that recognize its approval: Belgium, Croatia, Czechia, Denmark, Estonia, Latvia, Lithuania, Slovakia, and Slovenia. That’s 10 countries counting the Netherlands, up from eight at the start of October.

The EU-wide decision still sits with the Technical Committee on Motor Vehicles (TCMV). The RDW says those talks are confidential and won’t comment on them. As we reported, the EU pushed the vote to December at the earliest, and passing it takes a qualified majority: 15 of 27 member states representing at least 65% of the EU population.

Speed has been the biggest problem for Tesla’s file in Brussels. In June, Sweden asked the EU to reject FSD unless Tesla removed the ability to set speeds above the posted limit. In September, a Brussels pedestrian safety group found the system speeding in 55% of the 30 km/h segments it tested, in a country that had already approved it. France has cited urban speeding as one of its reasons for refusing to recognize the Dutch approval.

Then, on October 6, Germany’s Transport Ministry said it would back EU approval after Tesla agreed to drop the “Full Self-Driving” name, which the ministry called misleading, and to cap speed tolerance at 10%. Today’s RDW update delivers both.

But 10% isn’t what Sweden asked for. Sweden wanted no setting above the limit at all. Several European countries are known for enforcing speed limits strictly.

Good, but that’s only part of the problem. Tesla’s biggest issue with FSD right now is that it doesn’t properly detect the speed limits.

Being locked at 10% over the limit doesn’t mean much if you don’t know the actual limit.

The name change is needed globally and it is past due. “Tesla Assisted Driving” describes what the system does. Meanwhile, Tesla still sells the exact same kind of software in the US as “Full Self-Driving (Supervised)” for $99 a month. If it’s misleading in Berlin, it’s misleading in California.

Practically, this update gets Tesla Germany’s vote. The question for December is whether a 10% cap is enough for France, Italy, and Spain, or whether Sweden’s zero-tolerance line wins out.

With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. Google will let schools skip AI tools, but they have to give up search, too

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Google will offer schools AI tools that allegedly don’t give away the answers.

While Google is trying to move beyond Chromebooks, the ubiquity of these devices has made the company’s education products huge in schools. Now, Google says it will let educators choose whether to allow its AI tools in the classroom. However, blocking AI tools also means giving up search, leaving schools with a difficult decision.

Google’s new overarching approach to AI in the classroom sounds good on the surface. After consulting educators, researchers, and lawmakers, Google has decided that schools using its education products will be opted out of AI tools by default. It will be up to administrators to manually enable them when these changes roll out in the coming months.

When you look at what Google considers to be an AI tool, the nature of the Faustian bargain becomes clear. The affected products are Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom, and Google Search. There’s no picking and choosing—you either allow all the AI or none of it.

This is actually consistent with Google’s approach in the consumer space. Google executives have said in no uncertain terms that Google Search is AI search. Most searches now provide an AI Overview at the top, and the company is constantly coming up with new ways to nudge people toward the conversational AI Mode chatbot function. Google sees this as the future.

In a statement to The New York Times, Google education VP Chris Phillips reiterated this approach to web search. “Search and AI are deeply integrated,” he said, adding that if a school chooses not to enable AI, the students “will not have access to search, and they’ll see a message from their school that indicates it’s not available.”

Google could make a version of search for schools that doesn’t have AI tools, but it’s not doing that. Instead, it’s creating two different versions of AI in search. When schools enable AI for student accounts, they’ll have two options: the standard Gemini-based AI and the new “Guided Learning” version. The latter, available in Classroom as well as search, is supposed to provide one-on-one tutoring and assistance without giving away all the answers. Google notes all of its generative AI products can make “mistakes,” though, so it may not be difficult to talk the chatbot into doing your homework.

Google is betting that schools won’t want to give up Google Search and will therefore accept AI tools. This will expose students to AI early and often, possibly changing the way they interact with technology. Schools that don’t like Google’s approach could move to alternatives like DuckDuckGo, which allows you to disable AI features. But will Google’s new Guided Learning be enough to assuage their concerns?

Weight-Space Programming Works: An LLM Fixed Qwen's Math Loop by Editing 0.016% of Its Weights

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 3 • Originally published at dispatch-blog.hashnode.dev

One hundred thirty-one thousand numbers. That is the entire size of the edit that moved Qwen3.5-0.8B, a small open reasoning model, from 7.9% to 48.1% on MATH-500, from 13.8% to 67.1% on GSM8K, and from 21.0% to 62.3% on IFEval, an instruction-following benchmark. No gradient descent was involved, no training data, no fine-tuning run. A frontier model called GPT-6 Astra read the model's raw weights, identified a single direction written by two MLP layers that was trapping the model in repetition loops, and subtracted it. The whole repair touched 0.016% of the network's weights.

The result comes from "Sparks of Weight-Space Programming," a November 2026 research post by Rikiya Takehi, Jayden Teoh, Alex Zhang, and Omar Khattab at MIT CSAIL. The claim behind it is larger than one clever repair: a general-purpose language model, used off the shelf with no task-specific training, can read neural network weights well enough to say what a network was trained to do, and can write weights well enough to beat reinforcement-learning policies trained for hundreds of millions of steps. The post is careful, the logs are public, and the failure cases are reported next to the successes. It deserves a close read, so here is one.

The authors give the idea a precise definition. In weight-space programming, a general-purpose LLM reads and writes a network's weights directly, not through an optimizer, and in particular not with gradient descent, working toward a natural-language specification, sometimes with no environment feedback at all. Three capabilities get tested in turn. Reading: given only the weights, say what the network was trained to do. Fixing: given a failing network, find the cause and repair it. Writing: given a task description, produce a working network from scratch.

The contrast with business as usual is the point. Today, weights are written by symbolic optimizers like Adam or Muon, by secondary networks trained to generate them, or by compilers from formal programs. Humans cannot meaningfully edit a weight matrix by hand, which is exactly why weights have remained the exclusive territory of those algorithms. The post argues this assumption is now breaking: the same reasoning ability that lets a frontier model debug code transfers, at least partially, to the continuous space of network parameters.

The first experiment is a clean test. Four policies were trained with PPO to play four different games, grid versions of Breakout, Pong, Pac-Man, and Freeway, on the same 10x10 grid with the same five actions and identical architectures: an MLP of shape 500 to 32 to 32 to 5, just 17,253 weights, trained for a billion steps each. Astra received the raw weights as text plus a description of the grid and the actions, without the list of candidate games. It named the right game for all four networks. Its reasoning, summarized in the logs, is the kind a human expert would attempt: the first layer's weights concentrate where each input channel matters, so for Breakout the agent channel's weights lie along the bottom row where the paddle lives, and the output layer separates different actions for different games.

The second experiment moves to generative models: three small diffusion models trained to draw circles, squares, and triangles on 12x12 grayscale images, 23,824 weights each. Astra named each model's shape from the weights alone, with no tools and no forward passes. The tell that it was reading rather than simulating: it drew every shape as an outline, while the actual models generate filled shapes. It was reading the geometry of the output layer, where each hidden unit's weights trace one segment of an edge, not replaying the network in its head.

The third experiment is the strongest read, and the one most worth your skepticism. Three character-level transformers of 7.16 million parameters were trained on a billion characters each of Python code, romanized Japanese literature, and English Wikipedia. Astra received only the four middle transformer blocks, no input embedding and no output layer, so nothing in what it saw maps any dimension to any character, plus a Python session for analysis and a hard ban on running the network on any input. It named the right language for all three models, in both of its attempts, and it listed every copying head, the induction heads that let a model repeat earlier text, matching the measured ground truth exactly: heads 2.1, 3.1, and 3.5 for Python, 3.3 for romaji, 3.1 for English. To do this it independently re-derived the induction-head analysis from Elhage et al.'s well-known interpretability work, a method nobody mentioned to it. The romaji detail is my favorite in the post: the final block Astra was given writes in fewer distinct directions, exactly what you would expect from an alphabet that never uses q, l, v, or x.

Reading is a parlor trick if it does not generalize to writing, so the authors escalated. For three MinAtar games, Breakout, Seaquest, and Asterix, Astra received the rules and source code, a fresh network of shape 600 to 32 to 32 to 6 with every weight set to zero, and nothing else: no data, no loss, no gradients, no tools, no rollouts in the environment. It wrote all the weights as text in a single reply. The results, scored over 1,000 episodes each:

Against PPO trained for 100 million steps, the hand-written networks win all three games. Against PPO trained for 500 million steps, they score twenty times higher in Breakout, come close in Asterix, and reach 60% of the trained score in Seaquest. How does it do it? It writes programs in neural form: for Breakout, it assigned the first layer to compute where the ball will land and the second layer to move the paddle to that position. That is not a metaphor. It is a hand-coded algorithm expressed in matrix weights.

The Go experiment then pushes the scale up. The target network for 9x9 Go has 1,480,274 weights, far too many for the context window, so a Python sandbox held the tensors while Astra edited them with numpy, unable to run the network except through a budget of forward passes, each one a batch of up to 128 practice games. With a budget of zero forward passes, meaning a single Python run of 336 lines, the authored network already won more than half its games against two smaller PPO networks. With 50 forward passes, it beat an equal-sized PPO network trained on 20 million games. The program it wrote is recognizable Go: early layers mark each side's stones and their liberties, middle layers propagate along chains to find groups in danger, and the final layer scores each move, capturing here, saving a group there, avoiding self-atari, never filling its own eyes.

The repair experiment sits between reading and writing. A PPO-trained car policy for a Kinetix ramp level, 171,664 weights and a billion training steps, had converged to a local minimum: it drove straight at the ramp and stalled, succeeding zero times in 256 episodes. Given the weights, a description of the scene, and one recorded episode, Astra explained the failure and rewrote 3.5% of the weights in a single reply. The car then succeeded in all 1,024 evaluation episodes. The mechanism it described is surgical: it added a small side path, a few units per layer, that reads the car's position and speed, triggers the back-up-and-accelerate behavior, then switches itself off once the car clears the obstacle so the trained policy drives the rest.

The headline experiment scales the repair to a real language model. Qwen3.5-0.8B, with thinking enabled, is prone to repetition loops; its own model card warns about this. The setup gave Astra a Python kernel over the model's weights with no GPU, practice problems drawn from the GSM8K and MATH training sets (7,469 and 7,275 problems, disjoint from every test set), and a budget of 10, 25, or 50 forward passes, where one forward pass means the model answers up to 512 problems or reads up to 1,024 texts. Training and gradients remained forbidden. Sampling followed the model card exactly: temperature 0.6, top-p 0.95, top-k 20, presence penalty 1.5.

The failure mode was unmistakable once looked for: 85 of the base model's first 96 practice answers ran all the way to the 32,768-token limit, repeating themselves and never reaching an answer. Astra traced the looping to one direction written by the MLPs of layers 15 and 19, and subtracted that direction from 64 output columns in each layer, 131,072 weights in total.

The measured outcome, on benchmarks Astra never saw:

The loops are not specific to math, so the gains are not either: IFEval, which measures instruction following, nearly tripled. Lower budgets barely helped, since at 10 or 25 forward passes the loops mostly survived, which is itself useful information about how much interaction this kind of repair currently needs.

The post earns unusual credibility from its negative results. In the sycophancy experiment, two copies of Gemma 3 270M were fine-tuned identically, one to answer correctly and one to always agree with the user's suggestion, with embeddings and output layers frozen so the input-output distributions stay identical. From the weights alone, Astra failed: across four attempts it found many weight differences but could not map them to behavior. Only when the authors relaxed the setup, letting Astra write up to 16 texts of its choosing and inspect only the attention patterns of both models, never their outputs, did it identify the sycophant, correctly, in all three pairs. Its explanation was mechanical: in the sycophant, middle-layer heads shift attention from the question's content to the user's suggested answer. One head, 10.2, moves from spending 94.3% of its attention on the question's numbers in the honest model to spending 85.7% on the user's guess in the sycophant.

The second failure is the control experiment everyone should cite. Gemma 3 270M rarely loops, only 8.5% of its answers hit the token limit, so the authors asked Astra to simply make it better at math. It could not. Reading its failures, Astra found ordinary reasoning errors, wrong arithmetic and invalid algebraic steps, with no single component responsible for them. Its edits stayed small and targeted, and none moved MATH-500 by more than about a point, a gain Astra itself reported as not established. The boundary this draws is the most important sentence in the post: editing weights can remove an obstacle that hides an ability, but it cannot add an ability the model lacks.

Against fine-tuning, the Qwen repair wins on three axes and loses on one. It needed no training data and no gradient pipeline; it touched 0.016% of the weights, which sidesteps the catastrophic forgetting that broad fine-tuning invites; and the improvement generalized sideways to instruction following instead of trading one capability for another. What it loses is reliability and cost structure: the repair worked because the failure happened to have a single-direction cause, and 50 forward passes through up to 512 problems each on a frontier model is not free, merely cheaper than a training run.

Against mechanistic interpretability, the shift is one of reader, not target. The field has built excellent purpose-built tooling, from probes to circuit tracing to feature dictionaries, and more recently has trained models to read activations or explain single neurons. What is new here is that the reader is a general-purpose frontier model with no interpretability-specific training, working in natural language over raw tensors, and reproducing a known research method on its own. The sycophancy negative result matters here too: pure weight reading has a ceiling, and pairing the reader with a lightweight behavioral probe, the attention tool, is what broke through it.

Against reinforcement learning, the from-scratch results are genuinely startling at small scale: one-shot authored weights beating PPO at 100 million steps in three games, and beating a 20-million-game PPO run in Go with 50 forward passes. But note the parameter counts, 17 thousand to 1.5 million. Nobody wrote a 0.8-billion-weight model from scratch; the language-model result is a repair, not an authorship. Extrapolating the writing results to frontier scale is speculation the authors themselves decline to make.

The authors list the applications plainly, and each is worth tracking as an engineering direction rather than a shipped product. Auditing without evaluation: reading hidden behaviors, backdoors or deceptive circuits, directly from weights, without needing a test set that anticipates them. Zero-data steering and unlearning: removing a capability by editing a handful of weights instead of collecting data and fine-tuning, with the rest of the model left intact. Targeted hotfixes: the looping repair is exactly the shape of bug that currently forces a retrain or an embarrassed system-prompt patch. And true model merging: grafting a circuit from one model into another of a different architecture, instead of averaging weights, which only works between identical twins.

The near-term checkpoint for all of this is public. The authors are building a benchmark and harness for weight-space programming, and they have released complete logs, every prompt, every reasoning summary, every piece of code, every network, and every evaluation, at github.com/RikiyaT/lm_understand-logs. The stated bottlenecks are context length and harness efficiency, and the stated scope is early. If the logs hold up under community re-runs, this is the beginning of a real engineering discipline; if they do not, it was still the most interesting negative result of the month. Either way, the honest move for practitioners is the same: watch the benchmark, try the harness when it lands, and keep your training pipeline warm.

Track hiring signals at any company with the Greenhouse and Lever public job APIs

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Thousands of companies host their careers page on Greenhouse or Lever. Both platforms expose a public, documented, read-only API for those job boards, meant for building job sites. That makes them a clean data source for a useful question:

Which companies are hiring, for which teams, and how fast is that changing?

Sales teams use it as a buying signal, investors as a proxy for momentum, job seekers as an alert feed. This post shows how to get it in a few lines of Python, without scraping HTML and without collecting any personal data.

Both return JSON lists of open postings. Lever asks clients to stay at about one request per second.

Calling them directly is easy for one company. It gets tedious when you want a watchlist, "only new jobs since yesterday", normalized fields across both platforms, and a per-company summary. That is what the Company Jobs Tracker Actor does, so the rest of this post uses it.

The full script formats it as a table. Output from a run on 31 October 2026:

Run it again tomorrow and each row also has newSinceLastRun, closedSinceLastRun and changeSinceLastRun.

The first run returns everything; every later run returns only postings that appeared since the previous one. Put it on a cron job or an Apify schedule and pipe the result to Slack or email.

Open the company's careers page and look for links to boards.greenhouse.io/, job-boards.greenhouse.io/ or jobs.lever.co/. You can also just pass the slug and the Actor checks Greenhouse first, then Lever.

Runnable examples: github.com/caspiandata/caspiandata-examples

Building Production Features with the Claude API in Java

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Most Java teams first meet large language models through a demo: a chat window, a clever prompt, a convincing answer. Turning that demo into a production feature is a different job. The model call becomes one more remote dependency with latency, rate limits, timeouts, and failure modes. Its output is probabilistic, so it needs validation before anything downstream trusts it. And it handles data that often belongs to customers, so security and auditability matter from the first request.

Claude, the family of models from Anthropic, fits well into this kind of backend. It is available through the Claude API, it supports tool use so the model can call your own functions, and it can process long documents and structured inputs. This article shows how to integrate it into a Java service in a way that you can test, observe, and operate. We will cover the request model, a minimal client, structured output with validation, tool use, error handling, and the operational concerns that determine whether the feature survives contact with real traffic.

The Claude API exposes a Messages endpoint. You send a model name, a token limit, an optional system prompt, and a list of messages alternating between the user and the assistant. The response contains content blocks, usually text, and metadata such as token usage and a stop reason.

Three headers matter. The x-api-key header carries your secret key, the anthropic-version header pins the API version so behavior does not change under you, and content-type declares a JSON body. Keep the key in a secrets manager or environment variable, never in source code. Use a dedicated key per service so you can rotate it without affecting other systems.

Model choice is an engineering decision. Smaller, faster models suit classification, extraction, and routing. Larger models suit multi-step reasoning and nuanced writing. Pick the smallest model that meets your quality bar, measure it, and revisit the choice as your requirements change.

Java 21 ships with a capable HttpClient, which is enough for a first integration without adding a dependency. The example below sends a single message and returns the text of the first content block. It uses a plain JSON string for clarity; in a real service you would use Jackson to build and parse the payload.

Two points deserve attention. First, the timeout is set on the request, not only on the connection, because a model call can legitimately take tens of seconds and a missing timeout can exhaust your thread pool. Second, the status check is explicit. A non-200 response should become a typed error in your domain, not a string that leaks into a user interface.

Free text is hard to use in code. For most backend features you want a predictable shape: a category, a list of fields, a score. The reliable approach is to ask for JSON, describe the exact schema in the system prompt, and validate the result before using it.

Ask the model to respond only with a JSON object matching this structure, then parse it with Jackson into the record. Validation is not optional. Check that the category belongs to your allowed set, that the priority is one of your known values, and that the summary is within your length limit. If validation fails, retry once with the validation error included in the prompt, and then fall back to a human queue. Treat the model as an untrusted input source, the same way you would treat a partner API.

Tool use lets the model request an action from your application. You declare tools with a name, a description, and a JSON Schema for their inputs. When the model decides a tool is needed, it returns a tool_use block. Your code runs the function, then sends a tool_result block back in the next message. The loop continues until the model returns a final text answer.

This pattern is powerful for banking, support, and operations workflows, where the model should look up an account balance or a ticket status rather than guess. Keep the tools narrow, read-only where possible, and authorize every call against the end user's identity, not the service's. A model should never be able to do something the requesting user could not do directly.

Set a maximum number of tool-call iterations, such as five, so a confused model cannot loop indefinitely and multiply your costs.

Calling a model is cheap for a demo and expensive at scale, so design for cost from the start. Measure token usage per request and log it with the feature name, so you can see which parts of the product drive spend. Prompt caching lets you reuse a long, stable system prompt or document across requests at a lower cost, which is valuable for support assistants that repeat the same policy text.

Rate limits and transient errors are normal. Implement retries with exponential backoff and jitter for 429 and 5xx responses, and respect any retry guidance in the response headers. Put a circuit breaker around the client so that a degraded provider does not drag your whole service down. Degrade gracefully: if the assistant is unavailable, show the standard workflow instead of an error page.

Observability is part of the feature. Track latency percentiles, error rates by status code, token usage, tool-call counts, and validation failures. A rising validation failure rate often signals a prompt regression or a change in input data before users notice.

Before sending any data to a model, decide what it may contain. Customer identifiers, account numbers, and personal data should be removed or pseudonymized unless there is a clear need and an approved basis to include them. Review your provider terms and your organization's policies for data retention and processing. In regulated industries such as banking, involve your security and compliance teams early, not after the prototype works.

Protect against prompt injection. Text from documents, emails, or web pages can contain instructions that try to override your system prompt. Keep untrusted content clearly delimited, never let model output decide authorization, and validate every tool call against the current user's permissions.

Test the parts you control. Unit-test the prompt builder, the response parser, and the validation rules with fixed inputs. Keep a small set of representative examples and expected categories, and run them as a regression suite whenever you change the prompt or the model version. Model outputs are not deterministic, so assert on properties, such as valid JSON, allowed categories, and presence of required fields, rather than exact wording.

For the client, use a fake HTTP server in integration tests to simulate timeouts, 429 responses, and malformed bodies. Your retry and fallback logic should be exercised without calling the real API on every build.

Claude gives Java teams a capable model behind a straightforward HTTP API. The hard part is not the first request; it is everything that surrounds it: validation, timeouts, tool authorization, cost control, and testing. Treat the model like any other critical dependency and design the boundary carefully, and it becomes a dependable part of your architecture rather than a fragile experiment.

How To Watch Other Mommy At Home

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This has been a great year for horror, from the monumental successes of "Backrooms" and "Obsession," to more high profile favorites like "The End of Oak Street" and "Resident Evil." We've seen independent productions make waves, established filmmakers making their comebacks, and much more. There have also been plenty of lackluster horror movies with just enough interesting ideas to strike a chord with audiences.

Case in point, "Other Mommy," a pretty okay horror film that features Jessica Chastain doing her best Pennywise. That's it, that's the whole movie. Adapting Josh Malerman's novel "Incidents Around the House," Rob Savage helms a stylish horror film that is carried entirely by Jessica Chastain's unhinged performance and a fair amount of violence and spooky stuff.

In spite of less than stellar reviews, "Other Mommy" was a hit with audiences. The movie rose above its abysmal Rotten Tomatoes and CinemaScore evaluation, audiences showed up to theaters, and "Other Mommy" became a box office hit. It's a testament to the marketing department and the trailers for the movie, which hooked audiences on the premise of Savage's film long before the reviews dropped.

Now that Halloween has passed, it's slim pickings for horror at the box office. The good news is that all the horror films released before Halloween are going to start being released on home video soon. Universal Pictures Home Entertainment has announced that "Other Mommy" will release on digital platforms November 10, 2026 with a Blu-ray release expected later, on December 29, 2026.

One of the more intriguing and also confounding aspects of "Other Mommy" is its titular character, whose strange powers seem like Pennywise, Freddy Krueger, and a whole bunch of nightmares combined. It makes her a bit convoluted, sure, but also one of the most exciting horror villains in a while.

It remains to be seen if "Other Mommy" will warrant a sequel, or if Jessica Chastain would return to deliver more horrors. In the meantime, however, you can watch more than what was in theaters, because "Other Mommy" is bringing deleted scenes and other bonus features when it hits home video.

Jessica Chastain stars in a terrifying trip into the darkening world of a young girl, Bela, who forms a relationship with a disturbing entity in her home who looks uncannily like her own mother. 'Other Mommy' proves to be more than a mere figment of the girl's imagination as she becomes a sinister, manipulative presence determined to tear apart Bela's family.

The following bonus content is available when you purchase "Other Mommy" at participating retailers.

EFFECTING FEAR: An exploration of the ways special effects complemented each other to amplify the relentless tension, suspense, and fear driving the story.

A FAMILY IN PIECES: A behind-the-scenes look at the fractured family at the heart of the film and the standout performances that bring them to life.

BREAKING RULES WITH OTHER MOMMY: Cast and filmmakers discuss subverting horror tropes through Bela's perspective and disrupting the expected cadence of the genre.

Medicare's CJR-X Mandate Shifts $392M From Hospitals. ROMTech®'s AI Helps Them Win It Back.

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

ROMTech's award-winning, AI-enabled rehabilitation targets the 90 days after discharge, where CJR-X results are decided.

BROOKFIELD, Conn., Nov. 3, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- On January 1, 2028, Medicare's CJR-X model puts 2,217 hospitals under mandatory two-sided risk for the full cost of hip, knee and ankle replacements through 90 days after discharge. Hospitals that beat a regional target price earn a bonus; hospitals that miss it pay Medicare back.

For most hospitals, the math points to paying back. CMS's final rule projects hospitals will repay $1.855 billion over five years while receiving $1.463 billion, a net $392 million transfer to Medicare. More than half of its projected $736 million in savings comes from pricing alone.

"Ultra-dense data reveals what no human eye can see." said Peter Arn, CEO of ROMTech. "ROMTech's AI predicts how each recovery will end, while there is still time to optimize the ending."

Newsweek named ROMTech one of only 10 winners worldwide of its 2026 AI Impact Health Awards, judged by leaders from Memorial Sloan Kettering, Hospital for Special Surgery and Mount Sinai.

For a hospital with 300 episodes a year at a $20,794 target, running 5% over costs about $312,000. At ROMTech's average savings of $2,400 per episode, it would save about $720,000 a year.

The long wait for All’s Fair’s second season is finally almost over. The Ryan Murphy legal drama is set to return on Wednesday on the 2026 TV schedule, nearly a year after Season 1 ended. However, can we please talk about Teyana Taylor’s fit, too?

On Monday, Hulu hosted a premiere for All’s Fair Season 2, and the fashion was absolutely on point. Konami might have finally set down the Metal Gear Solid Master Collection Vol 1 to focus on releasing Metal Gear Solid 4 from its PS3 prison, but modders continue chipping away at the PS2-and-earlier era Metal Gear games. MGSHDFix, whose mission statement is to repair broken visual bugs, fix crashes, and generally give the older Metal Gears a bit of a spitshine, has got a big new update. Now is the era of MGSHDFix 5.0 (actually, 5.0.1. There was a hotfix).

"We've got performance improvements, massive vanilla loading time reductions for MGS2, loose .MP3/.WAV/.OGG/FLAC soundtrack loading for MG1/MG2, WIP raw mouse input support, and loads of new restoration and bugfixes", writes author Afevis Solmunko.

There are, to be honest, way too many fixes to properly delve into here, so I recommend either checking the patch notes out yourself or taking the mod for a spin in your preferred Metal Gear. We can call out some of the big ones, though.

For example, want to play Metal Gear 2 as Mel Gibson? No? Well that's a shame, bucko, because MGSHDFix 5.0 provides an option to restore that game's MSX2 sprites. "Yes, you can finally experience Mel Gibson fighting Sean Connery as Kojima intended," writes Solmunko.

Also in the OG MGs, there's now an option "to make doors automatically check your inventory for keycards (how MGS2 keycards work)", which frankly sounds blasphemous to me.

More practically, the latest version of the mod boasts that it dramatically cuts down loading times in MGS2. "We've greatly sped up level loading by adding a new system that prefetches a list of all the assets for all the rooms that are directly connected to the current stage you're in… Speedrunners have reported that this new prefetch system has shaved upwards to 5 minutes off of the elapsed realtime from their runs". On Linux (and thus, Steam Deck) specifically, Solmunko says this can "reduce level transitions load times by 4~6 seconds for every room you go into".

Plus, MGS2 and MGS3 now have limited support for raw mouse input in third-person, with support for first-person hopefully coming in MGSHDFix 5.1.0.

All good news, in other words, and it's nice these classics are now released in a way that lets fans do this kind of loving work for as long as they like.

Apple Just Stockpiled $10 Billion of Parts While Warning Memory Prices Are 'Unprecedented.' Should Investors Worry?

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Apple's annual report shows it loaded up on components as memory costs soar. Here's what that means for its margins, and for the stock.

Apple (AAPL -2.08%) just revealed to investors that it's buying parts as fast as it can, and it is paying up big to do it.

The company's annual report, filed Tuesday, shows components inventory of $10.4 billion as of Sept. 26, up from $2.1 billion a year earlier. Meanwhile, finished goods, meaning iPhones and Macs sitting in warehouses, barely moved, rising to $4.1 billion from $3.6 billion. In other words, nearly all of the buildup in inventories is parts.

The filing came a day after John Ternus, on his first earnings call as CEO, said "What we're seeing with memory pricing right now is unprecedented" and warned that costs will climb further. Put the two together, and you get a clear picture of how the world's most valuable consumer company is handling a memory shortage.

The parts pile isn't the only sign. Apple had $89.7 billion in manufacturing purchase obligations at fiscal year-end, with $82.7 billion due within 12 months. A year earlier, that total was $56.2 billion. Ternus said those obligations rose by $33 billion in the September quarter alone.

Building that stockpile cost real cash. Inventory soaked up $8.9 billion of operating cash flow in fiscal 2026, after adding $1.4 billion the year before. Of course, Apple can afford it: The tech giant still generated $145.1 billion in cash from operations, and it spent $85.8 billion buying back its own stock.

The 10-K is blunt about why. Apple says it is facing supply constraints and rising costs for advanced chips, memory, and storage, and it "expects these trends to intensify for memory and storage components." It also warns that price increases "may not effectively mitigate these negative impacts, and may also reduce demand for the Company's products."

The cost is already hitting margins. Apple's gross margin was 47.9% in the September quarter, but that included about one percentage point from tariff refunds. Without those refunds, CFO Kevin Parekh said, margin fell about a point from the June quarter, "driven entirely by memory impacts."

The December quarter looks tighter. Parekh guided gross margin to 45%-46%, mainly due to higher memory costs and some spending to roll out Siri AI. Ternus added that memory prices should keep rising beyond December, while the cushion from cheaper parts Apple bought earlier shrinks.

Apple has already passed some of that on to customers. Parekh said the company "reluctantly" raised prices on iPad and Mac around June and on iPhone in September, and it's too early to say how buyers will respond.

Memory is also limiting what Apple can sell. Chief Operating Officer Sabih Khan said Mac and iPad supply in the December quarter will be held back mainly by memory constraints, as demand has run ahead of expectations.

Despite the frenzy around memory, there's one big bright spot for Apple: Product demand is certainly not a problem. Revenue rose 12% to a record $114.5 billion for the September quarter, and iPhone sales jumped 17% to $57.6 billion. Meanwhile, services grew 10% to $31.6 billion. For the full year, revenue climbed 15% to $478.8 billion and net income reached $132.2 billion.

Apple also expects December-quarter revenue to grow 7% to 10%, which Parekh said would make it the biggest revenue quarter in company history.

And the stockpile cuts both ways. When memory is scarce, the company that can commit tens of billions of dollars to suppliers in advance gets served first. Smaller phone and PC makers don't have Apple's balance sheet, so the same shortage that trims Apple's margin could cost them sales outright.

The memory squeeze is also a signal about who's winning right now. When Apple, one of the largest buyers of memory chips in the world, says prices are "unprecedented," that's good news for the semiconductor companies selling them, including Micron Technology (MU +2.32%).

For Apple shareholders, an important number to track is gross margin. If it stays near the 45% to 46% range and revenue keeps growing, the memory hit is a speed bump. If price increases start to dent iPhone demand, as Apple's own filing warns they could, that's a bigger problem.

So, is Apple stock a buy? The company is choosing to protect supply and sales over a point or two of margin, and it has the cash to do it while still paying a $0.27 quarterly dividend and buying back stock. For long-term investors, that looks like the right trade. Just don't expect margins to look as clean as they did last year until memory prices cool down.

Jonathan Ponciano has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Apple and Micron Technology. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Apple Stock Fell After a Record iPhone Quarter. Here’s the Margin Problem Investors Shouldn’t Ignore.

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Investors are looking past iPhone growth to how much profit Apple keeps on each device, and rising memory costs are pushing that down.

Apple (AAPL -2.08%) just delivered the biggest September quarter in its history, with revenue jumping 12% to $114.5 billion. Its iPhone business performed even better, and earnings per share topped Wall Street's expectations.

So why did Apple stock fall about 2% on Tuesday?

The answer has less to do with how many iPhones Apple is selling and more to do with how much money it's making on each one. Rising memory chip prices are putting pressure on Apple's profit margins, and management doesn't expect relief anytime soon.

I'm still bullish on Apple, but investors shouldn't dismiss this problem.

Apple's iPhone revenue jumped 17.5% year over year to $57.6 billion, beating Wall Street's estimate of $56.5 billion. That's an outstanding result for a product line that already generates more than $200 billion in annual sales.

Not everything went Apple's way. Its iPad revenue declined 10%, while wearables revenue fell 8%. Management attributed those declines partly to difficult comparisons with last year's product launches and to supply constraints affecting the iPad.

But the iPhone remains Apple's biggest business, and its performance suggests consumers are still willing to pay a premium for the company's flagship products. The problem is that Apple is paying more to make them.

Apple reported a gross margin of 47.9% for its fiscal fourth quarter of 2026 (ending Sept. 26), although that figure benefited from roughly 1 percentage point of tariff refunds. Without those refunds, its gross margin would have been approximately 46.9%.

For the December quarter, management expects gross margins between 45% and 46%.

That might not sound like much of a difference. But every percentage point of gross margin on $114.5 billion in revenue represents roughly $1.1 billion in gross profit. And Apple expects its December quarter to be even bigger.

The culprit compressing margins is memory chips, which are becoming increasingly expensive as demand for artificial intelligence infrastructure puts pressure on supply. CEO John Ternus described memory pricing as "unprecedented," while CFO Kevan Parekh said rising memory costs accounted for the entire underlying gross margin decline between the June and September quarters.

Apple has already raised prices on its iPhones, Macs, and iPads, but it's also benefiting from memory purchased earlier at lower prices. As those inventories are depleted, higher costs will increasingly affect profitability.

And there's another complication. Apple stock trades at roughly 35 times expected earnings, leaving less room for disappointing results.

I don't think rising memory prices fundamentally change Apple's long-term investment thesis.

For starters, Apple's services business carries a gross margin of approximately 76%, making it substantially more profitable than hardware sales. That business provides an important cushion against rising manufacturing costs, although management expects services revenue growth to slow to between 8% and 9% in the December quarter.

Apple also has the financial resources and purchasing power to secure memory supplies. Its manufacturing purchase obligations increased by $33 billion during the quarter, reflecting efforts to lock in future production.

That doesn't eliminate the margin problem, and Apple's Siri AI rollout will introduce additional costs. But memory prices are cyclical, and Apple has weathered component shortages and rising manufacturing expenses before. The bigger question is how much of those costs Apple can pass along to customers without hurting demand.

When Apple reports its Q1 2027 results, I'll be watching whether gross margins land within management's 45% to 46% forecast. I'll also be paying attention to services growth and whether supply constraints affecting Macs and iPads begin to ease.

Apple's iPhone business is performing exceptionally well, and I don't see any reason to abandon my bullish outlook over one disappointing margin forecast.

But at 35 times expected earnings, Apple needs more than strong sales. It needs to demonstrate that it can keep those sales profitable, even as the cost of producing its products increases.

Patrick Sanders has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Apple. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.
