Dopo un brutto ko nel derby contro il Lille di Davide Ancelotti, il Lens intende rialzare la testa e ripartire subito, per ovviare anche ai recenti risultati negativi maturati in Champions League. Solo una vittoria - contro lo Slavia Praga - poi ko con Sporting Lisbona e Club Brugge, domani sera al Bollaert-Delelis invece ospiterà il Como di Cesc Fabregas con ben 6 punti in tre turni in League Phase, al debutto assoluto nella competizione. L'allenatore dei Sang et Or, Yannick Cahuzac, ha parlato in conferenza stampa alla vigilia dell'incontro.

Prima di tutto il punto sulla squadra: "Odsonne Édouard è rientrato dal suo intervento. Maik Nawrocki, Ismaelo Ganiou e Abdallah Sima stanno proseguendo il loro percorso di riatletizzazione. Per il resto, ho a disposizione tutto il rimanente gruppo", ha informato il tecnico di 41 anni. Opinion: San Francisco is delivering, while Seattle is still deliberating

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

by Joe Nguyễn on Nov 3, 2026 at 9:47 amNovember 3, 2026 at 9:47 am

Editor’s note: GeekWire publishes guest opinions to highlight a range of perspectives on issues shaping the tech and startup community. To submit a guest column, email [email protected].

How can a region care so much, spend billions in tax dollars and yet leave people losing faith in its future?

In Seattle, that question has become painfully familiar. People see suffering and disorder on their streets with little reason to believe help is coming. Business owners wonder whether the city values their presence or understands what it takes to keep their doors open. Even people who love this place are beginning to speak about its problems as permanent.

That growing resignation took us to San Francisco. We wanted to understand how a city so many had written off was giving people reasons to believe in its future again.

I expected to come home impressed with San Francisco. Instead, I came home with a deeper appreciation for Seattle. And an anger I am still trying to reconcile.

Seeing San Francisco make progress reminded me that Seattle has the talent and resources to do the same, perhaps even better. That is what made me frustrated. We should not have to accept people suffering on our streets and businesses struggling to stay open as conditions beyond our ability to change.

Not long ago, San Francisco had become a warning about what happens when a prosperous city loses its way. Less than two years into Mayor Daniel Lurie’s administration, that story has begun to change. Reported crime fell 22 percent in the first half of 2026 compared with the same period a year earlier. According to Kidder Mathews, office vacancy declined to 26 percent in the third quarter from 31 percent in the prior year.

San Francisco still has profound problems, and the suffering we saw on its streets cannot be erased by encouraging statistics. But the people we met shared a confidence that their work could change the city’s direction. They understood what they were working toward and could explain what would happen next. Their optimism comes from seeing progress and believing they can build on it.

And I kept wondering why that felt so refreshing.

Seattle has extraordinary talent and resources, with public servants who care deeply and service providers doing demanding work under difficult conditions. Our business community helps shape the world. With that much ability and commitment here, we should feel confident that we can make our own neighborhoods safer and more welcoming.

Yet we often seem more resigned to our limitations than confident in our possibilities.

I recognize that habit because I have worked inside it. As a legislator and state agency director, I saw good intentions fall short when leaders avoided hard decisions and failed to hold people accountable for results. Even small obstacles became accepted excuses when no one took responsibility for overcoming them.

Throughout the trip, I kept looking for a more complicated explanation for the difference between San Francisco and Seattle. Eventually, the answer became impossible to miss.

Leaders expected the institutions they led to deliver results and took responsibility for making that happen. The unsettling part was how unfamiliar something so basic had come to feel.

San Francisco offered examples of what happens when leaders refuse to accept failure.

“Perhaps the most consequential thing we have lost is the expectation that we can do better.”

One example was Mayor Lurie’s response to open-air drug markets. The previous administration had already brought police, public health officials and other agencies together, but people we met described meetings that produced more updates than action. Lurie took that existing structure and gave it a clear purpose. Participants were expected to make decisions and take responsibility for carrying them out, then return with evidence of what had changed on the streets.

This approach helped illuminate a problem that is also familiar to Seattle. Officers encountering someone dangerously intoxicated often have no appropriate place to take them. Leaving the person on the street puts them and others at risk, while taking them to jail does little to address their need for care and would remove scarce law enforcement officials from patrol for hours.

San Francisco opened the RESET Center to fill this gap. Eligible individuals are able to receive brief medical supervision and stabilization, followed by connections to services. The center opened within ten months of the decision to proceed. It is a limited response to a larger crisis, but it gives people an option they lacked.

Across our conversations, leadership meant staying with a problem until the next step became possible. That requires something more uncomfortable than calling for accountability. It requires accepting that evidence may challenge an approach we have previously defended.

If we believe Seattle is a progressive city, we should ask whether our policies deliver the progress we promise. That means questioning our assumptions and changing programs when results fall short. Reconsidering an approach can feel like abandoning our values, but those values should compel us to change when people are still in need of help.

The business community must reconsider its own habits too. We often avoid politics even as public decisions shape our future. In San Francisco, business leaders helped set the agenda and backed leaders willing to act. If we want better outcomes here, we must help shape the decisions that produce them.

Government, in turn, must make clear that businesses belong here. A shop owner asking for a safe doorway and a person in crisis outside that door both deserve a response. A capable city should be able to care for both.

People form their judgment of a city through these encounters. They notice whether someone answers. They notice whether anything changes.

Over time, those experiences become confidence or despair.

San Francisco reminded me that resignation can lift. People can begin expecting more when they see reasons to believe their efforts matter.

Seattle has so much worth believing in. What we need is leadership willing to turn that promise into something people can feel.

Perhaps the most consequential thing we have lost is the expectation that we can do better.

What might we accomplish if we recovered it?

Elon Musk’s Tesla and SpaceX Block Their Stock From OKX-ICE Tokenized Venue

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Elon Musk’s Tesla and SpaceX Block Their Stock From OKX-ICE Tokenized Venue

Two of Elon Musk’s companies are now keeping their shares off OKX and ICE’s planned tokenized stock venue.

Tesla and SpaceX are among six new objecting issuers listed in an updated notice from OKXICE TSV dated Monday. Amazon, Netflix, Lam Research, and SoFi Technologies are the others. The additions bring the total to 56 companies.

The list stood at 50 in the venue’s Oct. 30 notice, when Nvidia, Costco, and Nike led the biggest batch yet. That followed 38 on Oct. 28, after Walmart joined, and nine on Oct. 15, when the first objectors appeared.

The new names deepen the roster of megacap holdouts. Amazon, Tesla, and Netflix join Microsoft, Alphabet, and Nvidia, while SoFi, a fintech company that offers crypto trading, adds a company with its own stake in digital assets. SpaceX, which trades under the ticker SPCX, rounds out the batch.

Under the SEC’s innovation exemption, which the agency unveiled in September after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate, a venue must notify a company before offering a version of its shares tokenized by an unaffiliated third party.

If the company objects in writing within 30 days, the venue can’t make that stock available for trading, according to the SEC’s order. The objection applies only to that venue.

OKXICE TSV, a 50-50 joint venture between NYSE owner Intercontinental Exchange and OKX’s U.S. holding company, plans to offer tokenized U.S. stocks 24/7 through permissioned Uniswap v4 pools on OKX’s XLayer blockchain, paired with stablecoins such as USDC.

To trade, users must pass identity and sanctions checks and hold a nontransferable “soulbound” token that the venue’s smart contracts verify before every transaction.

Apple is now one of the few megacap names still missing from the list. And while objections bar OKXICE from listing third-party tokenized versions of a company’s stock, they don’t close every door.

The notice says the venue can also list shares tokenized by the issuers themselves, though it doesn’t identify any, and that tokenized stocks it offers aren’t restricted to trading on OKXICE alone.

Floating Alone Down the Brahmaputra on a Bamboo Raft

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Indian mountaineer and adventurer Shekhar Bordoloi has just started a solo expedition down the Brahmaputra River. He set off on a homemade on a bamboo raft from the Dhola–Sadiya Bridge on November 1 and aims to finish at Dhubri, 891km later. The route covers the entire Brahmaputra within India and should take 22 to 25 days in all. The river begins in Tibet and continues beyond India into Bangladesh, covering 2,900km in all.

This won’t be Bordoloi’s first time down the river. In 2020, he and two friends took a similar bamboo raft down a 400km section of the Brahmaputra. Earlier, in 2015, Bordoloi was one of 11 who tackled another part of the river over 11 days under the auspices of the Assam Mountaineering Association. This expedition, however, will test his endurance, pitting him against river conditions for longer, and with less support, than ever before.

Bordoloi on a previous raft expedition on the Brahmaputra. This raft took Bordoloi and friends from the world’s largest river island, Umananda, to its smallest, Majuli. Photo: Shekhar Bordoloi/Instagram

Bordoloi’s raft was hand-built from bamboo poles carefully lashed together over several days. Such craft are a traditional way of navigating rivers across the region, and are still used by local communities for fishing and transportation.

Though the river can be temperamental, Bordoloi’s first two days have been relatively calm, taking him down the Lohit, one of the Brahmaputra’s many tributaries and channels. Navigating the complex river system alone will be a challenge. The Brahmaputra is one of the largest river systems in India, the ninth-largest in the world by discharge, and is prone to flooding and powerful currents.

But according to Bordoloi, those obscure, winding corridors are at the heart of this project. Many sections of the river remain underexplored, and he believes there is hidden potential for the people in his home region of Assam. If he can map routes for river tourism and model environmentally responsible river navigation through those routes, he hopes to draw attention to this important riverine ecosystem and the economic opportunities for the people living along it.

The logistical challenges of a long expedition will further complicate progress. On previous expeditions, Bordoloi traveled with minimal supplies or no food at all, relying entirely on foraging and knowledge of the natural landscape. But this adventure will take over twice as long and may put those skills to their limits.

Bordoloi is documenting his daily progress on Facebook and Instagram.

Lou Bodenhemier holds an MA in History from the University of Limerick and a BA in Creative Writing from the University of Arizona. He’s interested in maritime and disaster history as well as criminal history, and his dissertation focused on the werewolf trials of early modern Europe. At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.

Ex-XRP Treasury Reveals Exit From $5 Million Stake in Ripple

📅 2026-11-03  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

A fresh annual report of British company VivoPower PLC, filed with the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) on November 2, 2026, officially disclosed the parameters of the closure of its cryptocurrency strategy.

The documents recorded the creation and subsequent liquidation of a $5 million stake in the private company Ripple Labs, while also making public the price of their buyback at $300 apiece.

According to the filing, in May 2025, VivoPower announced a large-scale treasury expansion with a target investment volume of up to $100 million in XRP tokens and Ripple Labs equity. In practice, the investments turned out to be more modest: the XRP treasury allocated $5.0 million to the direct acquisition of 41,810 Ripple Labs shares in January 2026.

For institutional investors, buying stakes in the fintech giant itself was a key way to make a large bet on the ecosystem's success.

However, the experiment lasted only nine months from its announcement. As early as February, VivoPower's board of directors decided to fully wind down the crypto direction. Management recognized holding digital assets as less profitable than developing artificial intelligence infrastructure and redirected capital to the purchase of a 41.5 MW data center in Mo i Rana, Norway, for $37.7 million.

The process of liquidating this $5 million stake brought the crypto market an important price insight. In April 2026, Ripple Labs conducted a targeted buyback, taking 882 shares from VivoPower at $300 apiece. The deal brought the British company $264,600 in cash and $159,122 in net profit, giving the community a rare and hard marker of how Ripple's management itself values its business in private trades.

A direct sale of the remaining part of the stake on the open market proved impossible due to the non-public status of the securities. As a result, VivoPower entered into agreements to sell the economic rights to the shares to two South Korean partners, KWeather Co. and Lean Ventures.

As of the filing date, the Korean deals have not yet been legally completed. The remaining 40,928 shares are locked in the Korea Securities Depository (KSD) under a strict one-year lock-up restriction. Due to this freeze and the lack of market liquidity, independent appraisers lowered the book value of this stake to $4.3 million (about $106.16 per share).

The VivoPower story clearly showed the crypto industry the flip side of institutional investments: a direct purchase of stakes in Ripple Labs comes with strict lock-ups and low liquidity, where the only fast source of fiat for exiting the ecosystem is the issuer itself through a private buyback procedure.

Bethesda has officially christened the next entry in its long-running fantasy RPG series The Elder Scrolls 6: Sentinel. This is our first meaningful bit of news on the game since the first brief teaser trailer the studio unveiled in 2018, but keep your hopes in check: even with the arrival of the series' 40th anniversary, this doesn't mean that Sentinel's release is imminent.

I hope Sentinel lives beyond those sorts of comparisons, but Bethesda has a lot to prove after Starfield disappointed so many of the studio's longtime fans. Kicking out veteran developers certainly won't make delivering on those expectations any easier.

Shopify Stock Just Plunged. Here's Why I'm Not Selling

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Shopify's third-quarter results were better than you might think.

Shares of Shopify (SHOP -5.47%) fell by about 10% on Tuesday morning after the e-commerce platform reported its third-quarter results. The drop left the stock roughly 22% below its October record. As a Shopify shareholder myself, I obviously don't enjoy watching the stock price fall. But after going through the report, I'm not planning to sell a single share.

Here's what happened, and why I think the market's reaction says more about expectations than about Shopify's business.

Shopify's revenue grew 32% year over year to $3.74 billion. Merchant solutions revenue, which mostly comes from payment processing, rose 35%, and subscription revenue grew 20%.

The company's profits grew even faster. Operating income jumped 66% to $568 million, and free cash flow came in at $816 million, or 22% of revenue, up from 18% a year earlier. Shopify also spent $562 million buying back its own stock. On the bottom line, Shopify posted earnings of $0.49 per share (adjusted), which surpassed analyst estimates.

Based on the last section, you might be wondering why Shopify's stock is under pressure. And the reason is that two metrics came in a bit light. First, gross merchandise volume (GMV), the total value of sales on Shopify's platform, rose 29% to $119.1 billion. That's an impressive growth rate for a platform this size, but it was slightly below analysts' expectations and slower than the 32% growth rate Shopify reported in the second quarter. Subscription revenue of about $836 million also fell short of the consensus estimate of roughly $850 million.

Normally, misses this small wouldn't be that concerning. The problem is that Shopify's stock was priced for near-perfection. As of Monday's close, Shopify traded for roughly 64 times analysts' 2027 earnings estimates heading into the report. With that kind of valuation, investors tend to punish anything short of perfect. We've seen this with several other big-name tech stocks that have fallen after strong earnings and guidance.

Speaking of guidance, management expects fourth-quarter revenue to grow in the low thirties (percentage) and gross profit in the high twenties. It also expects operating expenses to fall to 29% to 30% of revenue, from about 32% in the third quarter, and it guided to a free cash flow margin in line with last year's fourth quarter. In other words, Shopify expects to keep growing at about the same pace through the holiday season while becoming more profitable.

On artificial intelligence (AI), Shopify's Harley Finkelstein said the company is building commerce "into every surface, every agent, and increasingly, everywhere."

I've owned Shopify since 2022, and my thesis rests on three things. I want rapid growth, expanding margins, and a platform that gets stronger as more merchants and shoppers use it. This quarter checked all three boxes. A company growing revenue by about a third while increasing its operating income by two-thirds is doing what I want it to do.

To be sure, the stock isn't cheap even after the drop, and the two metrics Shopify missed expectations on are certainly a concern worth keeping an eye on. If GMV growth continues to slow, the valuation could keep shrinking, and that's the main risk I'll be watching over the next couple of quarters. The same goes for subscription revenue.

Having said all that, I don't think a slight GMV miss in one quarter changes Shopify's long-term path, nor does it make the company 10% less valuable than it was yesterday. I'm holding my shares, and if the price keeps falling, I'm more likely to add than to sell.

Matt Frankel, CFP® has positions in Shopify and has the following options: short January 2028 $170 calls on Shopify. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Shopify. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

‘Just Before Dawn’ Producer Prevails in Cult Horror Courtroom Drama

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A surreal legal battle unfolded last month in a courtroom in West Palm Beach, Fla., where a retired accountant faced off against a distributor of cult horror DVDs over ownership of a 1981 slasher film.

The distributor, David DeFalco, goes by “The Demon” and taunted the accountant in a series of unhinged livestream videos, calling him and his lawyers “fucking maggots.”

“We’ve got to defeat these scummy bastards,” DeFalco told his fans. “They’ve put huge damage to physical media.” Related Stories Netflix Sets Release Date for Rob Lowe Documentary 'The Outsider’ Why Did Netflix Boot Bela Bajaria?

That the case went to trial at all is unusual, and a testament to both sides’ commitment to principle regardless of legal costs. For the accountant, Vlado Hreljanovic, it was about reclaiming title to a movie he had made with his father, a Yugoslav producer who helped bring Milos Forman to the U.S.

Hreljanovic was young man when he put together financing for “Just Before Dawn,” a low-budget horror film set in the Oregon wilderness and starring George Kennedy. The film, which has grown in esteem over the years, was called “one of the most underrated slasher flicks of all time” by Horrornews.net and appeared on Variety‘s list of The 15 Scariest Films You’ve Never Seen.

The rights have remained in the family, so Hreljanovic was startled to find, a few years ago, that the film was streaming on Shudder, the horror site, and that DVDs were selling on Amazon.

“I never sold any of this to any of them,” he says.

That discovery led to four years of litigation that culminated on Oct. 20, when a six-person jury found that DeFalco had infringed on Hreljanovic’s copyright.

DeFalco is a former wrestler and director of “Chaos,” a 2005 film that he calls “the most violent film ever made” (and which Roger Ebert called “ugly, nihilistic and cruel — a film I regret having seen.”) For DeFalco, the fight was about protecting the beleaguered cult DVD ecosystem from nefarious “copyright trolls.”

Hreljanovic, now 79, spent most of his career in other businesses and is now retired. In 2012, he was approached by Bill “Banana Man” Olsen, who ran Code Red DVD and wanted to license “Just Before Dawn.” Hreljanovic agreed to a six-year deal, but was unhappy with having to chase him down for statements and decided to let it lapse in 2018.

DeFalco was a partner of Olsen’s, and had launched his own horror DVD business, Dark Force Entertainment. By 2022 — four years after the contract had expired — Olsen was ailing and DeFalco was trying to scrape together money to pay for Olsen’s caregiver.

“He gives me the title,” DeFalco says. “He says go license it to Shudder for two years.”

“These things don’t do huge numbers,” DeFalco tells Variety, saying the “Just Before Dawn” deal reaped $10,000. “You have to put out tons of them. Do you think any of these old B-movies have a huge value in the marketplace?”

Olsen died before the suit was filed, so Hreljanovic went after his estate and after his brother, Walt, who ran Scorpion Releasing. He also named AMC Networks, which owns Shudder, as well as Kino Lorber, Amazon, Target, Best Buy and Walmart. Walt Olsen died a few months later, and the other defendants reached out-of-court settlements — all except DeFalco.

DeFalco took it personally that Hreljanovic would come after him and after the Olsens, his mentors in the DVD business.

“I’m willing to destroy myself to get at you guys,” he said on a livestream. “This is going to be the war of the century.”

DeFalco hyped up the dispute for his online fanbase. Wearing a white tank top and long black braids, he unleashed expletive-filled rants against Hreljanovic and his lawyers, calling them “fucking maggots.”

“Why would you mess with the Demon?” DeFalco asked in one video, brandishing a blade. “I carve you to pieces with this!”

Behind him, a man in a skull mask could be seen racking a shotgun. “This dude is a killer,” DeFalco said. “You want to fuck with us?! You really think that’s a good idea?”

DeFalco’s legal argument was that Hreljanovic could not establish a chain of title to “Just Before Dawn.”

“I don’t really know who the fuck owns it at this point, but I can tell you one person that don’t own it, and that’s fucking Vlado,” he said in a video. He also called the movie a “little shitbag, piece-of-garbage movie that his garbage-ass father made.”

Hreljanovic had never met DeFalco and didn’t know quite what to make of it.

“His language is very rosy,” Hreljanovic says. “He threatened me that he’s going to bankrupt me… He slandered my sister. I’ve never known this guy. I have no idea where he came from.”

DeFalco ran up bills of about $200,000 on his legal defense. By the time of the trial, he was representing himself. He was not sure he would be able to do it, but he relied heavily on AI to draft his briefs — even winning a favorable pretrial ruling.

He showed up to court with his braids, black shirt, and black shoes with flames on the side. It caught the eye of the courtroom staff and of the opposing side.

At the trial, Hreljanovic’s lawyers sought to introduce the videos of DeFalco taunting their client — but the judge didn’t allow it.

“I can’t even remember doing this,” DeFalco says. “I was going on and on. I was calling him every name in the book. They edit it in such a way to make it look like I was threatening them. I never threatened these guys.”

Hreljanovic’s lawyers said that a key document — purportedly assigning the rights from Olsen to DeFalco — had been forged. And they also showed documents establishing that Hreljanovic still owns the copyright.

“We proved the chain of title going back 45 years,” said Eric Lee, Hreljanovic’s lead trial attorney. “That was the most important issue to my client.”

After two days of testimony and a day of deliberations, the jury found in Hreljanovic’s favor and awarded $46,000 — less than the maximum $150,000 — in statutory damages.

DeFalco has no plans to pay, and is preparing to ask for a new trial. “There’s certain things the AI is putting together to file the motion,” he says, adding that he still plans to beat Hreljanovic “into submission.” But as he turns his attention to a new streaming venture, he is done with DVDs.

“I’m getting rid of Dark Force,” he says, saying his “nice little lifestyle business” had been dealt a setback by the litigation. “I’ve had it with physical media.”

Hreljanovic is happy that “Just Before Dawn” still has a cult following, and says he plans to bring out a 4K restoration next year. He says the trial was “an expensive exercise” and is relieved it’s over.

United Airlines CEO Insists He Runs The “World’s Best” Airline. Does That Title Even Matter?

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Scott Kirby believes United Airlines is the best airline in the world (he has for quite some time). I understand his argument, and there are aspects of United that compare favorably with just about any competitor. But the claim raises a more interesting question: what actually makes an airline the best? The answer depends on much more than the seat or the route map or any other single quality…and maybe that’s asking the wrong question anyway.

In his recent Cheeky Pint interview with John Collison and Ben Thompson, Kirby made a case for United that deserves more than an eye roll.

His argument is not that United serves the best food or offers the most luxurious seats. He acknowledges that Asian and Middle Eastern competitors do better on the physical product and probably service. He also identifies schedule as the primary reason people choose an airline. But he believes United’s technology changes the overall assessment:

“I say schedule is the number one reason people pick an airline. But, everything else matters for brand-loyal customers. It’s service, product, reliability, and technology. The Asian and Middle East carriers do better than the US airlines on product for sure, and probably service too. I think we do a really good job, but they have different cultures for service, so they do better on that. But I think technology is the differentiator.

“United, that’s why I now believe that United in totality is the best airline in the world, and it’s because of technology. It’s exponentially getting better. Things like being able to do this for customers when there’s a delay and tell them what’s going on. We started it this summer. AI allows us to institutionalize this. But if you’re going to have a delay, like sending customers weather radar maps. In Chicago, there was a day where the weather was over the horizon, but you had to fly. It was sunny at the airport, but you had to fly through this line of thunderstorms, and so the routes were closed. You couldn’t—

“We sent half the people the normal weather delay, which they think you’re lying… Because it’s sunny outside. You must be lying to me. We sent half a picture with the radar map, and you have to fly through this line of thunderstorms, and the routes are closed because of outside. The people with a 1-hour delay, I think the numbers were negative 60 NPS on the normal way of sending it, plus 60 when we send a picture of the map. A 120-point difference.”

His case for United rests on technology: better tools for managing travel, clearer communication during disruptions, and connectivity that makes time onboard more useful. For example, he describes sending passengers radar maps to explain why distant thunderstorms were delaying flights even though the weather at their airport looked fine. His point is that passengers respond much better when they understand what is happening.

Thus, Kirby is acknowledging that competitors outperform it in some traditional measures of onboard quality, but arguing that United’s technological advantages outweigh those shortcomings.

I think he is right that these things matter. I am less convinced that they settle the question.

Start with the most obvious consideration: an airline is not particularly useful if it cannot conveniently take you where you need to go. I love trying new products and will sometimes take an absurd routing to do so. Most travelers understandably do not approach their trips that way.

A nonstop flight at a convenient time can be more valuable than a better seat reached through a connection. Frequency matters too. An airline with several flights a day gives you more options, particularly when something goes wrong.

This is a substantial part of United’s appeal. Its network gives me reasons to fly it that have nothing to do with whether another airline serves better Champagne.

But the best network for me may be largely irrelevant to someone living elsewhere. A traveler in Taipei and a traveler in Chicago are starting with very different choices. There is no universal route map that works best for everyone.

Emirates is a good example of why an airline’s reputation may need to be separated from the particular experience you are buying.

Mention Emirates and many people think of luxury (massive first class suites, showers, and bars onboard with beautiful flight attendants serving you extensive food and beverage offerings). That “halo effect” reputation is understandable.

But someone booking business class on an older Boeing 777 may encounter a product that falls well short of that image. The retrofitted 777s improve the situation with a 1-2-1 layout and direct aisle access, but I would not put that seat ahead of the leading business class products elsewhere.

Singapore Airlines illustrates this as well. Its reputation for service, built in part around the Singapore Girl branding, is extraordinary. Yet some travelers strongly dislike its longhaul business class seats, particularly their sleeping position.

A beautifully presented meal and gracious service cannot entirely compensate for a bed in which you struggle to get comfortable. Singapore’s new cabin reveal on Friday, November 6, will be particularly interesting for that reason.

My recent Emirates 777 first class flight demonstrated the reverse problem: a superb suite paired with service that did not live up to it. I loved the space, but the experience still fell short.

So what about Kirby’s argument that technology is most important?

We sometimes talk about technology as if it is separate from the passenger experience. But an app that lets you solve a problem without standing in line is providing service. A clear explanation of a delay is providing service. Working internet can determine whether a long daytime flight is productive or frustrating.

US airlines deserve credit here. Their digital tools can be excellent, and United’s app is absolutely the best in the business, no question about it. But even the Alaska, American, and Delta apps are–comparatively speaking–much better than their non-US peers.

To be clear, that does not mean every American airline leads every foreign airline or that an attractive app excuses indifferent employees. It does mean that evaluating service solely by how someone pours your wine misses a large part of what an airline does for its customers.

Connectivity is especially important to me. Extra legroom and a better meal are welcome, but painfully slow or non-functioning Wi-Fi can materially change how I feel about a flight. For someone hoping to disconnect and sleep, it may barely register. The features can be measured. The importance each passenger assigns to them cannot be universal.

I also recognize the limitations of my own perspective. Much of my coverage focuses on premium cabins, and those experiences influence my preferences. But what does the airline offer someone in economy?

Some Asian carriers offer a more appealing combination of legroom, meals, and attentive service than their US counterparts. That deserves weight, even if the business class cabin lacks the latest suite.

At the same time, the aircraft still matters. So do the fare restrictions, baggage allowance, and cost of choosing a seat. A generous meal does not tell us whether the ticket represents good value.

For a family paying for several tickets, the best airline may be the one offering a tolerable nonstop journey at an affordable total price. For a tall passenger, a little more legroom may matter far more than the entertainment selection.

An airline cannot claim superiority for everyone based on what it provides a small number of passengers at the front of the aircraft.

A great airline should operate reliably and treat passengers well when it fails.

Those are related, but separate, expectations. I want to arrive on time. When that is impossible, I want an honest explanation and practical assistance.

No airline can prevent every weather delay or mechanical problem. But how it prepares, communicates, and recovers tells me much more than a polished promotional video.

American Airlines is a useful example from my own flying. I would argue that some of my recent meals on American have been the best among the US network carriers. Its free Wi-Fi has also tends to work very well for me. Those are real strengths.

Nevertheless, my experiences with inconsistent service and operational problems place American well below Delta and United in my overall assessment. That is not a claim that every American flight is bad or every Delta flight is good. It is the cumulative effect of repeated experiences. A carrier earns my confidence over time, and it can lose it the same way.

The loyalty program belongs in this discussion too.

Award availability, redemption prices, and the usefulness of elite benefits all affect how much value I receive from an airline. A good program can persuade me to choose one carrier when the alternatives are otherwise similar.

But I try to separate the airline I enjoy most from the program in which I find the most value.

An excellent redemption does not make an uncomfortable seat comfortable. Status does not make a canceled flight depart. Conversely, a wonderful airline can have a loyalty program that gives me little reason to engage with it. Ideally, the program reinforces a relationship the airline has already earned through its service.

Qatar Airways and STARLUX are among my favorites because I have found their overall experiences so compelling. The appeal extends beyond a seat or a meal: I look forward to flying them because of the reliable Wi-Fi, excellent service, and quality in everything.

Air France has also come such a long way. Its newer business class seat is excellent, and the addition of Starlink on equipped aircraft addresses something that matters greatly to me. Combined with the food and service I enjoy, that makes it an increasingly attractive choice.

Yet I still have many reasons to fly United. Its network and technology have practical value in my life that an exceptional flight on another carrier cannot always replace.

That is why my favorite airline and the airline I choose for a particular trip need not be the same…maybe the point is that talking about which carrier is “best” is ultimately futile and our focus instead should be rewarding carriers who do things well and not patronizing those that continually disappoint.

Kirby’s claim is worth discussing because he recognizes that being the best involves more than having the fanciest cabin. Technology and network utility deserve more credit than they sometimes receive.

But I would not crown United the best airline in the world on that basis alone. The seat still matters. So does the person serving you, the reliability of the operation, and what happens when your plans unravel.

For me, the best airlines are the ones that bring those elements together consistently, but at the same time I realize how arbitrary my own evaluation can be. Thus, I tend to think asking “who is best” is the wrong question versus asking “who does X,Y, and Z well?”

What makes an airline the best for you—and which airline comes closest?

A Diablo 3 mechanic discarded because "it was not fun" found the perfect place in World of Warcraft

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A frustrating aspect of any creative process is having a good idea, but not the right means to execute it. Thankfully, if you're a studio the size of Blizzard, you can transfer mechanics across series if something's good but not quite fitting into the game it was designed for, which is how a notion for Diablo 3 wound up becoming central to an encounter in World of Warcraft.

Wyatt Cheng, a former game director at Blizzard, went through what happened on his Instagram account. In 2004, he was part of the devs working on Diablo 3, and they started experimenting with what set enemies might look like, as in ones that followed the logic of wearing green equipment to get an added bonus.

"Maybe a set monster pack is four enemies with one ability each, and each monster would grant its ability to the other three upon death," he explains. "We prototyped it internally. It was not fun."

A post shared by Wyatt Cheng (@candlesan)

He mentions that Diablo players don't tend to pay as much attention to the creatures they're battling on-screen, always going for the fastest kill and the best loot. The transfer of abilities would frequently go unnoticed.

Then, Cheng was transferred from Blizzard North after Diablo 3 was put on ice to assist in making the then-rapidly growing World of Warcraft. He was assigned boss encounters, and one of those battles happened to involve four beefy bros players would have to face, the Anubisath Sentinels at the Temple of Ahn'Qiraj. "Every single reason set monsters failed in Diablo was solved right here," he says.

To give some RNG, he made it so the Anubisath each randomly pick from a pool of eight abilities, forcing players to carefully assess each encounter. Everything fit, and Cheng learned that sometimes context is all it takes to make an idea shine.

Blizzard finally sees sense and caves to Diablo 4 players, increases Prime Evil Splinter stack size by 9900%

Pamela Anderson, 59, Pairs a Classic Red Manicure With a “Bombshell Bob”

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The actress makes a case for minimal makeup and statement curls on the red carpet

In 2023, Pamela Anderson famously vowed to wear less makeup on red carpets. Since then, the actress has attended countless events makeup-free—but she has never left her hair behind. In fact, Anderson, known for her iconic long blond hair, makes her hairstyle the centerpiece, as she did last night at the 2026 Council of Fashion Designers of America (CFDA) Fashion Awards.

Anderson walked the CFDA Fashion Awards red carpet in her typical minimalist makeup look, but with “bombshell curls” inspired by old Hollywood. In a black halter dress with feather detailing, accessorized with a just-bitten berry lip, Anderson wore a coiffed bob with old Hollywood pin curls. Because the curls fell away from her face, they showcased her glowing, fresh skin.

Anderson’s retro hair wasn’t the only statement look last night. She wore fall’s boldest nail trend—a bright, cherry-red almond manicure—contrasting with her satin black-and-white polka-dot clutch. With no shortage of buzzy new nail trends swirling around the internet, like “chili pepper nails” and “vanilla nails,” Anderson proves that sometimes the best shade for your manicure is classic red.

Lately, Anderson has given a master class in the many ways to style bobs. Just a few weeks ago, while at the 2026 Academy Museum Gala in Los Angeles, Anderson wore a head full of curls with subtle bangs and lots of volume.

In an interview with Anderson last year, she shared her hair philosophy—why she never sticks to one style. “I think people associate me with big blond hair, but if I look at my whole life, I’ve always been one to change up the way that I look,” Anderson told Bazaar. “Your hair is another way to express yourself, and I’ve never been shy.”

She continued: “I guess I’m genetically blessed, but I’ve always had very thick, healthy hair. I do like to cut it and to style it differently. It’s freeing. I am a big lover of change; that’s what keeps us young and excited.”

Class changes will come "over the course of the months and years ahead"

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

As World of Warcraft: Forever nears its big release tomorrow, Blizzard continues its work on the new MMO spin-off to address fans' growing feedback from the Beta – particularly when it comes to classes and their identities.

World of Warcraft has always done well to ensure every class feels unique and brings something to the table that others don't. It's the mark of a good MMO, after all, and Forever will offer no exception there. In the latest roundup of Beta development notes from Blizzard, the developers make as much clear, explaining that "trying to hold ourselves roughly to Classic power budgets for each class" hasn't been simple.

Every new addition came "at the expense of something else," and "those reductions left some iconic abilities feeling lackluster, and closed the door to other potentially exciting design options. So over the last two weeks, we've raised damage on classes across the game, and have increased the health of higher level creatures to compensate. This additional 'headroom' has allowed us to tune things to a place that we hope feels better overall."

As for this latest round of changes, it "reflects areas where we were likely hasty in turning to Burning Crusade – or Wrath-era solutions rather than carving out new ones that better fit into the Forever combat model. Our aim in class design is not perfect numerical equality, but rather for every class to feel valuable to the groups they join, with powerful, unforgettable moments brought on by mastering them."

In Forever, in particular, "this is accomplished by doubling down on the strengths and differences that make each class unique, rather than overly unifying them." Blizzard lists some of this "doubling down," so to speak, detailing changes to everything from the Druid (buffs and new passives, yippee) to the rogue. There's a lot, and that's putting it lightly, so I'd recommend browsing 'em all to see what your class will look like.

It won't all happen at once, either – there's more to come. "Once we have sufficient data from players leveling past 30 and engaging in a wide range of content, we expect that we will do a large tuning pass based on our data," as the devs admit. "And this is all merely a starting point for the classes in Forever, which will continue to evolve over the course of the months and years ahead just as they did following the original launch in 2004."

That's… pretty darn big, and I'm not alone in thinking so. Over on Reddit, it's clear that fans are happy with Blizzard's news – they just hope they stick by it. "I really hope they remember and stick to this throughout," one comment reads. "Let some classes be stronger for one raid, and then change things up for the next. As long as everyone gets their moment in the sun, I think some classes falling behind at times is a fair price for keeping things unique."

Weekly digests, tales from the communities you love, and more

I'd agree. Another response echoes this, calling the news "a massive W," while somebody chimes in, "They've learned. Amazing." I suppose all that's left now is to wait and see how it all unfolds come World of Warcraft: Forever's journey post-launch.

Here's how after 22 long years, World of Warcraft: Forever has finally sold me on Blizzard's undying MMO – and now I never want to leave.

AI-generated 'slopaganda' in political ads puts internet platforms on alert

📅 2026-11-03  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The midterm election cycle has been dominated by "slopaganda," or campaign ads featuring AI-generated clips and images, creating another challenge for Instagram, YouTube and X as they attempt to monitor their platforms.

The term slopaganda dates back to early last year, when a group of AI experts used it in a paper to describe a new phenomenon combining generative AI slop with propaganda and misinformation.

There's no comprehensive federal law governing the use or disclosure of AI in political ads. And while there are some 30 state laws with different requirements about AI use and disclosure, candidates don't always abide by them. On the leading internet sites run by Meta, Google and Elon Musk's SpaceX, users run into AI-generated ads and AI-generated posts shared by politicians, alongside authentic videos and clips.

More than $187 million has been spent on nearly 300 ads generated or enhanced by AI, according to an analysis released last week by the Wesleyan Media Project. That's almost certainly an undercount, the organization says, because so many ads don't contain disclaimers. Over 90% of the spending came from Republican candidates, a Republican party committee or a Republican-leaning group, the report found, and most don't include disclosures.

CNBC found a number of AI-generated ads running online without disclosures, including a fake video of New York Democratic Governor Kathy Hochul that was posted by her opponent, Republican challenger Bruce Blakeman.

In Texas, Republican senatorial candidate Ken Paxton created an AI-generated video showing Democratic opponent James Talarico making controversial statements, with a three-second disclosure flashing in the middle. It's being shared as organic content on YouTube and X by Paxton's campaign.

The Blakeman and Paxton campaigns didn't immediately respond to requests for comment.

YouTube told CNBC that the company applies "prominent labels to realistic AI content."

"For organic content on YouTube, political candidates can request removal where their face appears to be altered or generated by AI," the company said. "For advertisers, we require disclosure when an election ad contains synthetic or digitally altered people or events to provide viewers with transparency."

YouTube parent Google also requires political ads to complete its election ads verification process and include "paid for by" disclosures.

X says advertisers must comply with applicable laws, putting the responsibility on the advertiser, not the platform. The company didn't respond to CNBC's request for comment.

Meta referred to its guidelines on political ads, including restrictions on their use in the week prior to the election. The company also mandates "paid for by" labels and maintains an ad library of political ads. In June, the company ramped up its labeling, adding a new "about this ad" tool for ads that Meta detects have been created with third-party AI tools.

Last month, Meta was ordered to pay $25 billion in penalties by a New Mexico court as part of a data privacy case that stemmed from the company's Cambridge Analytica scandal from nearly a decade ago. Cambridge Analytica, which was a political consulting firm, allegedly accessed the information of 87 million Facebook users in improper fashion.

One risk the tech platforms face with generative AI is being asked to create misleading images and videos. The Brennan Center for Justice tested ChatGPT, Gemini, Grok, Claude, Perplexity and DeepSeek for their willingness to facilitate deceptive content. The study from August found that many models may avoid generating content around familiar conspiracies, but can generally be manipulated into creating misleading material.

A retest of those platforms in October found that ChatGPT had made progress. It had started redirecting prompts for harmful election images into illustrations so they look like comics, or refusing to take action on them altogether.

A number of national politicians are trying to address the issue.

The Protect Elections from Deceptive AI Act, backed by senators from both parties, would prohibit knowingly distributing materially deceptive AI-generated video or audio about federal candidates. Other bills are being led by Democrats in Congress, including the AI Ads Act, which is looking to expand current bans on campaign misrepresentation to include generative AI content.

None of the proposed bills have advanced out of committee.

How Hackers Stole $6.3 Billion From Binance: A Five-Month Plot Revealed

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Log in to access your notifications and stay updated. If you’re not a member yet, Sign Up to get started!

Writer by choice, CryptoCurrency Writer, and Researcher by chance. Currently, focusing on financial news and analysis, as well as cryptocurrency news and data. One may not call me a crypto “Enthusiast” but trust me I'm getting there.

Writer by choice, CryptoCurrency Writer, and Researcher by chance. Currently, focusing on financial news and analysis, as well as cryptocurrency news and data. One may not call me a crypto “Enthusiast” but trust me I'm getting there.

Writer by choice, CryptoCurrency Writer, and Researcher by chance. Currently, focusing on financial news and analysis, as well as cryptocurrency news and data. One may not call me a crypto “Enthusiast” but trust me I'm getting there.

Binance co-CEO Richard Teng said the hackers behind the record $6.3 billion theft spent months working their way into the exchange, rejecting claims that one compromised laptop was to blame. Speaking to CNBC, Teng said the attack began in June, when a wallet DevOps engineer installed a tampered version of Homebrew while searching online for a popular AI coding tool. Binance spotted the activity and replaced the device, but the engineer’s team had already been exposed. In September, the attackers posed as recruiters and gave another engineer a coding test containing hidden malware. They then moved through Binance’s internal AI tools to reach the system that approves wallet transactions. Teng said 98% of the $6.34 billion has been replaced. The FBI has linked the attack to North Korea’s TraderTraitor group, and about $3 billion has been frozen with help from Tether, Circle and Ripple.

Detroit Lions Coach Shares Update on Jack Campbell After Neck Injury

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

After Detroit Lions linebacker Jack Campbell suffered a major neck injury during the team’s game against the Minnesota Vikings, coach Dan Campbell shared an update on his recovery.

Days after the Detroit Lions linebacker suffered a neck injury in his team’s match-up against the Minnesota Vikings Nov. 1, his coach Dan Campbell shared an update.

“It’s not easy because he’s going to always be on our minds over here,” Dan, who shares no relation to his player, said while phoning into the 97.1 The Ticket Radio Show Nov. 3. “The good news is he is improving.”

The NFL coach—who has been with the Lions for five seasons—also shared an update on his player during a press conference for his team Nov. 2.

“It’s positive,” he said at the time. “Your prayers are working. Please continue to do that. We'll leave it at that, so continue to pray for him. But it is positive.”

Despite the Lions win against the Vikings 19-15 on Nov. 1, the team’s success was overshadowed by Jack’s terrifying injury, which occurred during the fourth quarter.

In footage from the game, Jack—who welcomed son Cooper with wife Megan Campbell in July—can be seen lying face down on the field, seemingly unable to move after tackling Vikings player T.J. Hockenson. The 26-year-old was quickly approached by medical personnel before being lifted off the field on a backboard.

Following the game, the Lions shared an update on the football player to X, writing in a post, “Jack Campbell is being transported to a local hospital to be evaluated for a neck injury.”

As of the evening of Nov. 2, the University of Iowa alum remained hospitalized.

“Jack Campbell continues to rest and recover at a local hospital,” the Lions wrote in a separate post on their X account Nov. 2. “Preliminary indications are positive and out of respect for Jack and his family we will not share further information at this time.”

While addressing Jack’s injury, Dan emphasized the value the player—who has played for the team since he was drafted in 2023 and was tapped as captain earlier this year—brings to the team.“I don’t even know if I can put it into words,” Dan said during a press conference Nov. 2. “You get voted captain, that says a ton, especially with this group of guys that has been assembled. He earned every bit of that. He’s trustworthy, he’s dependable. He’d give you the shirt off his back. He’s just all heart, man. All effort.”

The 50-year-old emphasized that Jack is “an unbelievable person.”

“Down to Earth,” he continued. “Very genuine. There’s not a man on this team that doesn’t respect Jack Campbell and care about him.”

The Jessie alum opened up about her "hellish" migraines, sharing how they've worsened after suffering five concussions over the course of her life.

"The brain injuries make things significantly worse," Ryan shared during a September 2026 episode of Mythical Kitchen’s Last Meals. "I had a pretty crazy, life-altering brain injury a few years ago, and I was out of commission for six months."

While Ryan described her first four concussions as "incidents," she said her fifth one was trauma caused by "sustained shaking over a minute of time."

Now, the former Disney Channel star needs to close her eyes to "think better."

"Quite often the world is really loud. It’s really too bright. There’s a lot going on," she explained. "The visual data that I'm taking in can be so distracting and so loud. Then, with the migraines—which are significantly worsened by that—it sort of is too much."

The Racing Louisville midfielder collapsed just a few minutes into her soccer team's September 2025 match against Seattle Reign at Lumen Field in Seatle.

The United States Women’s National Team player could be seen slowly sitting down on the field with a teammate by her side, per Reuters, as the referee called for trainers to check her out. She collapsed seconds later.

While the internet isn't always a kind place, Amy Schumer is happy that, in this case, it helped her get answers."The internet really came for me after doing a bunch of press, and I was like, 'OK everybody, relax,'" Amy recalled of her "puffier" face on a January 2025 episode of Call Her Daddy. Casey Fitzgerald's Neck Injury From Hockey Skate Blade

Hailey Bieber had the "scariest moment" of her life when she was having breakfast with husband Justin Bieber and started experiencing stroke-like symptoms in March 2022."Justin was like, 'Are you OK?'" the Rhode skincare mogul shared a month later on YouTube, "and I just didn't respond because I wasn't sure. And then he asked me again and when I went to respond, I couldn't speak. The right side of my face started drooping. I couldn't get a sentence out."While Hailey said the facial drooping stopped and her speech came back, she went to the hospital to make sure she was OK."They did some scans and they were able to see that I had suffered a small blood clot to my brain," the model added, "which they labeled and categorized as something called a TIA [Transient Ischemic Attack]."Later, Hailey went to the University of California, Los Angeles, where she found out she had a hole in her heart called a patent foramen ovale (PFO).She explained the blood clot had traveled into her heart, "escaped" through the hole and went to her brain, leading to the TIA.Hailey said she had a successful PFO closure procedure and was now feeling great.

So, there's full paralysis on this side of my face."In fact, he told his followers it had gotten "progressively harder to eat." But after a while, the paralysis went away."He's doing really well," Hailey said on a June 2022 episode of Good Morning America, later adding, "He's feeling a lot better. Obviously, it was just a very scary and random situation to happen, but he's going to be totally OK and I'm just grateful that he's fine."

Tracey Yukich's Medical Emergency on The Biggest Loser

The reality star revealed that she "died" while running a mile during the first challenge of the NBC competition series.

“I don't remember a lot,” she explained on Fit for TV: The Reality of The Biggest Loser. “I remember hearing the helicopter. I just felt like I was floating. And then my grandpa was there. And then I saw darkness. But then I saw light. So I knew, I knew I died that day.”

“My organs were literally shutting down,” she continued. “I didn't realize that I had rhabdomyolysis. And rhabdomyolysis is your body's way of saying, ‘I'm going to shut down on you.’”

Emilia Clarke filmed battle scenes for Game of Thrones, but in 2019, she published an essay in The New Yorker titled "A Battle for My Life."Having a bad headache at the gym, "I reached the toilet, sank to my knees, and proceeded to be violently, voluminously ill," the actress wrote. "Meanwhile, the pain—shooting, stabbing, constricting pain—was getting worse. At some level, I knew what was happening: my brain was damaged."She was taken to the hospital for a brain scan."The diagnosis was quick and ominous: a subarachnoid hemorrhage (SAH), a life-threatening type of stroke, caused by bleeding into the space surrounding the brain," the Emmy nominee added. "I’d had an aneurysm, an arterial rupture."Emilia had immediate surgery to seal the aneurysm, calling the pain "unbearable." While she was recovering, she continued, she experienced aphasia and was "muttering nonsense."A week later, "the aphasia passed," Emilia added, and she left the hospital a month after being admitted. At a 2013 brain scan, she learned a growth "doubled in size" and that she needed surgery again."When they woke me, I was screaming in pain," she wrote. "The procedure had failed. I had a massive bleed and the doctors made it plain that my chances of surviving were precarious if they didn’t operate again. This time they needed to access my brain in the old-fashioned way—through my skull."Thankfully, Emilia shared, she's now "at a hundred per cent."

Snoop Dogg's daughter Cori Broadus is grateful for her family.Because after she suffered a "severe stroke" in January 2024, her loved ones rushed to be by her side."I texted them that I just had a stroke and sent them a picture," she told E! News correspondent Will Marfuggi in December 2024. "Everybody just came to my rescue."Because of other medical concerns, Cori ended up staying in the hospital longer than she initially expected."I could have went home after my stroke, but my lupus wasn't doing so well," the Snoop's Fatherhood: Cori and Wayne's Story star continued. "My kidneys were failing. It was a lot going on."Today, Cori is on the mend."I'm doing great," she added. "I just had a little bit of motor skills that I had to work back on. But other than that, I was fine."

Shailene Woodley’s early 20s were not an easy time health-wise.“It got to the point where I was losing my hearing. I couldn’t walk for longer than five minutes at a time without having to lay down for hours, and hours and sleep. Everything I ate hurt my stomach,” the actress said on a September 2024 episode of the SHE MD Podcast. “It was this conflation of issues and diagnoses and different doctors telling me different things.”So Shailene—who chose to keep the exact condition private—set out to find answers. “I come from a very holistic background and study herbalism,” she added. “So I was very keen on, ‘I’m going to work with real MDs, [and] I’m also going to work with independent kind of healers.’ Just trying to search for some sense of comfort in my own skin.”It was a long journey—one that lasted for 10 years.“Throughout that decade, a lot of other things came from feeling so much discomfort physically,” the Big Little Lies star noted, “which was, ‘My gosh, if everything I eat hurts my stomach, I’m now suddenly afraid of food.’ And then going into the kind of mental f--ckery that can happen with that of body dysmorphia and confusion about identity and feeling safe in my own capsule, in my own skin, what that meant, and what that should be.”After tending to both her physical and mental health, Shailene is feeling much better.“It was a journey that ultimately physically resolved itself, and I am very healthy,” she shared. “I’m so happy to be able to say that."

Arc Raiders dev insists AI use was to save actors going "crazy if they had to read all of that"

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

One voice actor calls the AI text-to-speech tech "freeing" as "I can go and take a job somewhere else"

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Arc Raiders' audio director argues in favor of its AI text-to-speech, saying that "actors would just go crazy if they had to read all of that."

Arc Raiders came under fire last year by using AI text-to-speech for NPC characters found throughout the game, with Embark Studios co-founder Stefan Strandberg saying "there's no end goal in replacing any actors," and that the team used it to "maximize what we can, being a small team trying to get make rich worlds right, but still staying small."

Meanwhile, the CEO of Nexon said we should "assume that every game company is now using AI." Naturally, this caused backlash from players and developers, as these minor NPC roles were ones that could have been filled by professional actors, like NPC roles in countless other games.

In an episode of Embark's "The Evolution of ARC Raiders" video series (via Eurogamer) focusing on sound and music, audio director Bence Pajor recalls previous experiences in development when teams were unable to change gameplay decisions, calling it a "true pain." Pajor explains that while the training data for the text-to-speech was made with actor's performances, he argues that it wouldn't be feasible to have them record all of the lines, saying "the actors would just go crazy if they had to read all of that."

Diana Gardner who plays Celeste in the game, called the tech "freeing" as "I can go and take a job somewhere else," and if Embark needs her input, it'd be short enough that she doesn't have to cancel other gigs.

Despite arguments that using AI for performances does just that, Embark CCO Stefan Strandberg says "we are not in the industry of replacing actors," saying what the developers are doing is "the opposite." He explains, "We should celebrate the performance and give them more space. That's something I'm hoping to give to players over the coming years."

Arc Raiders devs know PvE has been "polarizing," promise to prioritize the game's "overall health and enjoyability" when deciding what to do next.

Five Ways North Korea Breaks Into Crypto Firms—and How Binance Was Hacked

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Five Ways North Korea Breaks Into Crypto Firms—and How Binance Was Hacked

Hackers from North Korea are notoriously effective at stealing crypto, with one estimate attributing 60% of all losses last year to DPRK-linked groups. These hackers enlist several different vectors of attack, making it all the more difficult to spot them in the act.

Last week, it took just 20 minutes for alleged North Korean hackers to drain $6.3 billion from Binance, in what is the biggest crypto hack on record. The attackers compromised a Binance engineer's work computer through a fake job interview, used an internal AI tool to gain control of other engineers' computers, and then used stolen credentials to hijack an internal wallet signing service, the exchange said.

In the past, North Korean hackers have been known to get hired at crypto companies under fake identities, attack through third-party infrastructure, and poison widely used code libraries. More recently, they've started using AI to make all of it more convincing.

Here are some examples of the methods that North Korean hackers are known to use in crypto attacks.

Binance chief security officer Jimmy Su told Decrypt in August 2025 that the exchange throws out suspected North Korean resumes every day, and that "almost all the large DPRK hacks," at the time, had involved a fake employee helping facilitate the attack.

Su said there were tell-tale signs in resumes that indicate the applicant was from North Korea, but the exchange was not willing to reveal the specifics on the red flags. Sometimes, though, malicious actors get through to the interview stage, where the attackers use deepfakes to change their face and voice.

"The only real good detection is that they almost always have a slow internet connection," he told Decrypt. "What's happening is that the translation and the voice changer are working during the call … that's why they are always delayed."

Those who do get hired tend to be suspiciously good at the job. Su said North Korean workers are often among a company's top performers, likely because several people may be sharing one role across time zones.

The threat has not let up. Binance said its security team detected and prevented 57 suspected North Korea-linked cyber threat cases in 2026, using techniques ranging from fraudulent job interviews to fake video conferencing apps.

Attackers also flip the scam around and pose as recruiters to target people who already work at high-profile crypto companies. Often, the attacker will claim there's a video or audio issue during the interview and send the victim a link to update Zoom, which infects their device with malware

"The DPRK group will [also] try to schedule calls with the external-facing employees," Su told Decrypt. "Either as a DeFi project or investment firm. Worst yet, they'll be recruiting them for a high-level job, paying twice, three times as much, just to get them onto an interview."

This is how the Binance attack began, according to the exchange. In September, a Binance software engineer was approached through a legitimate job-hunting platform by someone posing as a project director, who set up two interviews. In the first, a purported HR representative built trust over a live video call by sharing a social media account full of personal photos and videos, an approach Binance called "more advanced and deceptive than previously seen."

In the second, held on a chat app, the engineer was told to download an "assignment" onto his work computer. The code repository contained previously unknown malware that ran during setup, swapped a legitimate developer tool for a malicious version and connected to an attacker-controlled server, evading Binance's security tools.

One North Korea-linked group infected at least 30,000 devices in more than 100 countries between roughly December 2025 and July 2026 using similar methods. It took funds or credentials from more than 7,000 crypto wallets and moved around $10.71 million to North Korea.

The group, known as WaterPlum or Contagious Interview, posed as AI, crypto, or NFT companies and set candidates a technical interview or coding task that required downloading malware-filled files. Some used AI face-swapping software during interviews before cutting their video and asking the candidate to do the same, blaming a bad connection.

Binance's Su also told Decrypt that North Korean hackers poison public code libraries, ranking it as the second most common method of attack—behind fake employees.

Node Package Manager (NPM) packages are collections of reusable, open-source code that developers frequently use. Attackers can clone these packages and slip in a small line of malicious code while keeping its original function. That means that a victim could use this poisoned code library and not notice, with the malicious code embedding itself deeper over time as more is built on top of it.

In September 2025, software security firm ReversingLabs found two NPM packages that used Ethereum smart contracts to download malware, which the firm said it hadn't seen before. The packages were tied to a wider network of GitHub repositories, mostly branded as crypto trading bots, with thousands of commits and stars that ReversingLabs thinks were mostly faked.

The tactic formed part of a “sophisticated campaign” of malicious actors attempting to hack users via poisoned blockchain-related public code libraries, a vector of attack that Binance has previously attributed to North Korean hackers.

To counter the threat, Su said, Binance makes employees go through NPM libraries with a fine-tooth comb.

Tampered developer tools also played a part in the Binance hack. In June, a member of Binance's wallet DevOps team downloaded a tampered installation of Homebrew, a popular software installer, while trying to install an AI coding tool he had found through a search engine, the exchange said. Its security team caught the problem, replaced the device and rotated his credentials, but the incident exposed the engineer and his colleagues on the wallet infrastructure team to the attackers.

Before Binance, the unwanted record for the largest crypto hack belonged to Bybit, which lost $1.4 billion in February 2025. Cybersecurity firms revealed that North Korean hackers pulled off the attack by planting malicious code into the infrastructure of Safe—a crypto wallet provider used by the exchange.

The security firms said malicious JavaScript code was directly injected into Safe's online infrastructure. Though, it was unclear how the attackers managed to do this.

Bybit's infrastructure "was not compromised," the exchange told Decrypt, and the hack came purely because a third party was exploited.

The Binance attack targeted the exchange's own systems. On October 29, the attacker used stolen credentials to deploy a container and gain unauthorized access to an internal wallet signing service, which let it initiate transactions outside Binance's normal withdrawal process. On Ethereum, it used the signing service to exploit EIP-7702, a mechanism that lets an account delegate execution rights to a smart contract, giving an attacker-controlled contract partial control over a Binance hot wallet.

Those on-chain authorizations kept working even after Binance suspended withdrawals. Because the hot wallet used multi-party computation, there was no single private key Binance could use to revoke them quickly, the exchange said.

Every single one of the above mentioned attack vectors can be turbo-charged with artificial intelligence tools.

Early this year, Google warned of an AI-powered malware campaign targeting crypto companies coming from North Korea. Similarly, Binance's Su also mentioned that AI tools can be used to increase the effectiveness of attacks.

Concerns around AI-powered hacks have risen in recent months, with Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin saying in October that it's "plausible" AI could break encryption within the next two years. It came amid an explosion in AI-driven maths, with OpenAI claiming to have cracked a 90-year-old maths problem in a matter of days. Experts urged the industry to prepare for AI-powered attacks on encryption.

There are no confirmed reports of North Korean hackers using AI to break encryption. But given how quickly they've adopted AI elsewhere, the industry may have to prepare for that next.

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

If you purchase an independently reviewed product or service through a link on our website, Rolling Stone may receive an affiliate commission.

Keeping up with the news is as crucial as ever, especially on Nov. 3 as the 2026 midterm election results roll in. CNN remains an authoritative source of information, and tonight, the network is broadcasting live coverage of the polls.

Anderson Cooper and Jake Tapper will lead Election Night in America, the network’s official event broadcast, which promises in-depth analysis, expert panels, and commentary. They’ll be joined by fellow anchors Kaitlan Collins, Dana Bash, and more.

Looking to stream CNN online? Here’s a brief guide on how to access CNN through digital platforms, including an option to enjoy CNN content for free.

If you don’t have cable, the best way to watch CNN online is to get a live TV streaming service that carries the channel. DirecTV, Hulu + Live TV, and Sling are three of our favorite streaming services that offer CNN livestreams. Here’s a breakdown of pricing and free trial information for these services.
