This Mid-90s Austrian Car Company Cobbled Together A Lineup With A Lightly Rebadged Ford, Mercury, Saturn And Dodge

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sauber isn't a strange way to say Saab, also a defunct auto brand. Nor is it in any way related to the Sauber racing team that finally closed shop in 2025, giving way for Audi to take a seat at the Formula 1 table. The Sauber we are talking about today was an extremely short-lived Austrian car brand that existed from 1993-1995, solely to import American cars to Austria to sell them under the Sauber name. Well, it was a little more complicated than that. Its relative obscurity sent YouTuber corsicar on one of the most amusing missions of discovering what exactly the brand was, and more importantly, "why?"

Prior to corsicar's deep dive into this mysterious car company, knowledge on Sauber had been little to none. It seemed to have made it into the curious car chasm in 2025 via TheTruthAboutCars, and an Autopost article done shortly after. One simply cannot internet search Google themselves into a hole for hours taking in information on Sauber otherwise. Coriscar, however, found a newspaper ad for a vehicle called a Sauber Villager. It looks strangely similar to Mercury's Villager. Almost identical, because it is the same vehicle. This gets the whole search rolling.

The Sauber brand was established by a married couple in Austria in 1993. After getting things situated in Austria, the owners founded another company in the U.S., in Florida, Sauber Automobiles USA, Inc. This was likely to get around import laws and accruing unwanted taxes.

According to a review Coriscar found of the Sauber Villager in Auto Revue magazine, the Florida office was used to purchase American vehicles that would then be registered in the U.S. From there, it could export those vehicles to Austria, where they'd get a light facelift to make changes to meet Austrian regulations, and with a little rebadging, they'd officially be "Sauber" cars, as demonstrated by the ads found and shared in the video.

The company essentially tried to make a complete lineup of in-demand or soon-to-be in-demand vehicles using different American market models. There was the family hauler, minivan or MPV Sauber Villager, which was the Mercury Villager, a Ford product, which was also a Nissan product. Sauber's mid-size sedan, coupe and or wagon, the Messager, was General Motors' Saturn S-class sedan, 2-door coupe, and wagon. The entry-level econo-car was the unfortunately named Sauber Teenager, or a Ford Aspire. And the sporty car was the Sauber S1, which was a Dodge Stealth or Mitsubishi 3000GT.

Sauber models went on sale in 1994 with 12 existing dealers. The hope was to expand to 30 by mid-year. At those dealers, interested buyers could consider the cheaper, poorly named Teenager at around $14,500 (in 1994), the Messager at $22,000, the base Villager at $41,600, its top-of-the-line First Class trim level cost around $54,700, and the S1 offered at $76,100. Just for kicks, to give you an idea of how much that costs today with inflation, the Teenager would start at $33,000 and the top of the line, sporty S1 would be just shy of $175,000.

To get the attention of potential buyers, Sauber really played with the Austrian sense of humor. One ad shown in the video reads (in Austrian) "In life you have to try everything at least once. A test drive in a Sauber doesn't hurt either." Pictured below the text was a Sauber Villager (or basically edited Mercury Villager), and above the text, a pair of boxing gloves, cigars, and a leather crotch harness for you know exactly what.

It seems the ads were not funny enough because in May 1995 documents show the Commercial Court of Vienna rejected the car company's intent to declare bankruptcy, and instead officially declared the company dissolved. Sauber was so broke, it had nothing to help pay off debtors in a bankruptcy. What ruined it isn't clear. Importing cars from another country is an expensive endeavor. Buying a car to then make costly adjustments to them to meet regulations likely didn't help either. The whole model doesn't feel very viable. It also happens that a new tax introduced in 1994 — the Austrian Value-Added Tax Act — would have subjected those imports to heavier taxation.

Also, spending $100,000 (today) on a minivan is outrageous. I can't imagine the pricing did them any favors when there were plenty of vehicles in Europe, as well as imported for a price that was much more palatable for European families.

The whole thing almost doesn't seem real, but coriscar prudently shared every bit of archived paperwork, magazines, and ads he finds to verify that Sauber was indeed real, including a 1994 review of the Villager in Austria's Auto Revue. He actually bought almost all of the Auto Revues he could find just to find this one real-life review.

Corsicar was also able to find out through extensive research that the brand had maybe sold 28 cars in that time. In reality, it's still more real than Tesla's lastest Roadster might ever be.

If you do have any bit of a soul, the last part of corsicar's video will tug at your heartstrings. The YouTuber looked into how many of these nearly extinct relics had stayed on the road by looking at Sauber registrations in Austria. The last one on the road was in 2018. By 2019, not a single Sauber remained in motion — at least in Austria. If one has survived, we'll only know if you post a photo of it in the comments.

Epidemic of Hacked Freezers Spreads Across Europe

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Epidemic of Hacked Freezers Spreads Across Europe

Europe's freezers have gone rogue, with supermarkets in nine more countries reporting refrigeration systems that heated up overnight on Monday.

Stores in Belgium, Estonia, Finland, Ireland, Italy, Luxembourg, the Netherlands, Portugal and Sweden reported problems, and Estonian units were pushed to 50°C (122 °F).

No group has claimed responsibility, so the only ones taking the heat are the freezers.

The outbreak has a patient zero in France, where Finance Minister Roland Lescure told Parliament on Oct. 13 that about 700 of the country's 10,000 supermarkets were affected.

According to France's CERT-FR, hackers forced a continuous defrost cycle, which spoiled food and sparked fires at some stores. CERT-FR said it was aware of numerous compromises of remote control modules made by Danfoss and Copeland.

Groupement Mousquetaires, which owns Intermarché and Netto, counted 300 stores affected, while Cooperative U said 80 of its 2,000 shops were hit. Lescure said food supplies were not at risk but acknowledged the attack "touches the heart of our food security." Some Mousquetaires display cases were still out of service that Tuesday.

Monday's wave hit several chains in Belgium, where Carrefour reported disruption at about 15 of its 700 stores. Sabcobel, a Belgian refrigeration contractor, said evaporators had been switched into defrost mode, and federal prosecutors opened an investigation. Four Delhaize outlets in Luxembourg also reported problems.

Italy's Radenza Group, which runs Coop-branded stores in Sicily, said the attack began at 4 a.m local time. and targeted the remote-control system for frozen food sections. Ireland's Musgrave counted 13 SuperValu stores affected, and Mosqueteiros confirmed incidents at seven Intermarché stores in Portugal.

At 50°C (122 °F), a freezer is well past hot-tub territory and turns basically into a low-powered steam oven or, worse, a bacteria incubator where germs grow quickly if food is left there too long.

In Sweden, Coop said six of its roughly 800 stores were hit and that they ran on 4G connections, making them "more exposed." Ica said a limited number of stores had problems and that the affected shops used an external Danfoss control system. Neither chain has confirmed a cyberattack, and Finland's Kesko says network interference has not been ruled out.

EU Agriculture Commissioner Christophe Hansen called the incidents a "deliberate attempt" to disrupt the food supply chain. Investigators have not said whether Monday's wave is linked to the French attack.

Estonian refrigeration firm Kitman Coldtech said the affected units were older Danfoss models similar to those attacked in France. CERT-FR warns that leaving an end-of-life device without security patches exposed to the internet carries a very high risk of compromise. Researchers at Claroty published flaws in Danfoss AK-SM 800A controllers in August, including an authentication bypass.

Claroty found 2,765 of those devices exposed online, and Danfoss fixed the flaws in firmware R4.3.1. It remains unclear which weaknesses the attackers used, and CERT-FR says the campaigns are probably still ongoing, while Italy's government was set to hold a cybersecurity meeting on Tuesday.

For now, CERT-FR advises operators to take the controllers off the public internet, and Sabcobel's CEO says many older units need physical modifications that require investment. Until those arrive, there is no real fix for the oldest units, which makes your 1998 chest freezer the most secure device on the continent.

Meta pays up to borrow $15B as investors grow wary of AI debt

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Its 30-year bonds pay well above the deal it sold in April, as Big Tech floods the market with debt.

Meta is selling at least $15B of dollar bonds in a deal expected to reach at least $20B with a first euro note, Bloomberg reports. It is offering much higher yields than in April, as investors balk at Big Tech’s flood of AI debt.

Meta is selling at least $15B of US dollar bonds, its first debt sale in six months, Bloomberg reported on Tuesday, citing people familiar with the matter.

The two-currency deal is expected to raise at least $20B in total. It includes Meta’s first euro-denominated note, which could be sold as soon as Wednesday.

Meta is paying more to draw buyers. Its 30-year bonds are being discussed at about 2.5 percentage points above US government debt. That is nearly 0.9 points more than the early pricing on a similar bond Meta sold in April.

“We see no need to chase it in the new issue market,” said Ross Pamphilon of Impax Asset Management. He expects Meta and its rivals to keep spending heavily until at least 2027.

Big Tech has sold almost $430B of high-grade bonds since last year. Their bonds now trade at the widest gap over other high-grade debt since 2003. Alphabet paid up for a €6B euro sale on Monday, after Microsoft’s $25B deal last week.

Meta is also selling shares. Last week it priced $25B of common stock and $12.5B of preferred shares, part of its record equity raise.

The bond sale follows S&P’s move on Friday to cut its outlook on Meta’s credit rating over its AI spending.

Microsoft has listed some concrete ways that Windows 11 is getting better with 8GB of RAM

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

You should start noticing improved performance in a wide range of apps.

Microsoft has been doing a whole lot of shouting recently about how its 8GB Windows 11 PCs are performing better than ever, and it's now shared some more specific information about where you might start to notice a difference.

As previously revealed, the company claims that 8GB PCs are now up to 20 percent more responsive and 40 percent faster at multitasking. Windows is now able to retain up to 2GB more of your active data, rather than moving it to slower storage, which helps your PC stay more responsive as more tabs appear in your browser.

Since January, Microsoft says that interactions like opening Start, File Explorer and Settings, as well as searching and right-clicking, are all up to 20 percent more responsive, which it has achieved by directing your computer to reserve tasks like search indexing while you're working. Apps like Edge can also open up to 33 percent faster, while Efficiency Mode in Teams is turned on by default on 8GB PCs. This can speed up launch by up to 20 percent by dynamically reducing the load on your system resources.

Microsoft also says it has improved multitasking workflow speeds by up to 40 percent, which it tested by opening 20 tabs in Edge, as well as a 4K video, the Outlook app, File Explorer, and large files in Word PowerPoint and Excel. These were all left running once opened, and while 16GB of memory or more is still recommended for processor-hungry creatives who can afford it, you should be able to get more done quickly on a humble 8GB PC.

Apps like Word, PowerPoint, Excel and Outlook now use up to 360MB less RAM, which Microsoft says is down to improved efficiency, and even third-party apps like WhatsApp are hogging less memory than before. Other RAM-saving methods include Widgets that only load when opened and inactive tabs going to sleep after 15 minutes by default on 8GB configurations.

Aside from Windows itself, Microsoft says that newer processors are already playing their part. For example, a 2026 8GB Dell XPS 13 delivers over 90 percent faster multi-core CPU performance than its 2021 predecessor. The same 8GB model was able to largely match a 2021 configuration with twice as much memory in general CPU performance, web browsing, 4K video export and some AI tasks.

These performance and memory efficiency improvements require your Windows 11 PC to be running version 26H2 on the build 26300.9818 or later. Microsoft also recommends keeping all your apps updated and sometimes these improvements roll out through them.

GTA 6 Pre-Load Times, Install Size, And Global Release Times Confirmed

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Grand Theft Auto 6Grand Theft Auto VIGTA 6Pre-Load

We’re just over two weeks out from the launch of Grand Theft Auto 6 and Rockstar Games has finally revealed all of the details people need to know about getting the digital-only open-world game installed on their gaming console of choice.

The studio also confirmed that, yes, GTA 6 has a whopping 212GB install size, so start making room for that and god help you if you have an Xbox Series S. Here’s everything you need to know about getting GTA 6 ready to play for its release on November 19, 2026.

Digital pre-loading on PlayStation 5 will start globally on Tuesday, November 10 at midnight local time. On Xbox Series X|S, pre-loading begins globally on Tuesday, November 10 at 12 a.m. GMT, which is 7:00 p.m. ET on the previous day, November 9.

If you pre-order GTA 6 digitally, you’ll have access to pre-load the game immediately. If you are waiting for one of the codes in a box to ship to your home or be picked up in store, those will become available starting November 12. You’ll be able to take that code, redeem it, and immediately start pre-loading the game.

That’s a big file size, and console servers are going to get hammered when everyone starts trying to download it at once. To put it in perspective, 212GB is roughly one third of the available storage on a base PS5 and roughly a quarter of the available storage on a 1TB model. On Xbox Series S, it’s closer to half. In addition to taking some time depending on the speed of your internet, you’ll want to make sure you have your library cleared of any non-essential games. Good luck to you if you have children who are constantly swapping between a dozen different games every day.

The global release times for when GTA 6 goes live were previously announced, and unfortunately the U.S. is one of the last time zones to get access to the game.

Yes, the game will have been out in some parts of the world for roughly 18 hours by the time GTA 6 goes live stateside. My advice is to just stay logged out that day.

Grant Kirkhope Rayman Legends Retold - The Big Interview

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sam Woods is TheGamer’s Senior Reporter. He’s been in games journalism since 2020 and has undertaken a variety of roles in that time. These have included roles such as SEO Editor, Managing Editor at DualShockers, and Global Head of Gaming, and he's also been seen on the BBC. He was promoted to the Senior Reporter position in 2025 following a series of high-quality reports and exclusives for TheGamer.

Since 2020, Sam has written over 3,000 articles and specializes in telling the stories of the people behind the games. He’s interviewed the biggest game directors, some of the industry’s top talents, told the stories of how indie games came to be, and spoken to players about what really makes them tick. He’s also interviewed some of the world’s biggest musical artists to find out how the medium has inspired their work, and TV and Film actors about their roles in video game adaptations. He even hosted a PAX panel in 2021 and made a documentary in 2024 surrounding the creation of a Disney game.

Alongside this, Sam focuses on breaking news. He got the scoop on Perfect Dark’s cancellation, provided context on some AI drama, and revealed that Lady Dimitrescu’s actress would love to step back into the character’s giant shoes. These articles have been picked up by the biggest gaming sites, including IGN, GameSpot, and PC Gamer. He also loves to dive into the numbers behind the games.

Sam’s journey in games journalism has taken him all over the world. He’s covered Gamescom in Germany, Summer Game Fest in Los Angeles, and Tokyo Game Show in Japan, each on three occasions.

He’s been learning Japanese since 2024 with the intention of identifying and translating Japanese-language news and bringing it to English-language audiences first, something he’s done countless times.

Sam’s first review was 2020’s Battletoads reboot, and he’s since reviewed games in The Legend of Zelda, Super Mario, and Pokemon franchises.

There aren’t many composers in the video game space as esteemed as Grant Kirkhope. He’s won an Ivor Novello Award, and chances are he's composed music for at least one game you’ve played.

Starting at Rare in the mid-90s, his credits include series like Donkey Kong, Viva Piñata, Banjo-Kazooie, Minecraft Dungeons, Street Fighter, World of Warcraft, and Mario + Rabbids.

His latest adventure comes on Rayman Legends Retold, where he is composing for the game’s new world, The Land of the Livid Dead. Ahead of its release next month, we sat down with Kirkhope to discuss working on Rayman, his unique sound, and his illustrious career.

Kirkhope’s music traditionally has a lot of signature tells. He often combines woodwinds, strings, and xylophones to create whimsical soundtracks. Like Kirkhope, Rayman has sounds that are synonymous with the character. Did this process, merging two unique sounds, present a challenge?

“I guess because me and Christopher Héral worked together on the Mario Rabid Sparks of Hope Rayman DLC, we had an understanding. And so I think Ubisoft kind of spotted that our music kind of matched... We both write that kind of quirky music in a different way, and it kind of meets in the middle somewhere,” he tells me.

What about the character, is Rayman somebody that Kirkhope enjoys working with, given it's his second spell with the Ubisoft mascot after the Mario + Rabbids Sparks of Hope: Rayman in the Phantom Show DLC?

“It’s fantastic. Back in the day when I was at Rare, we were looking at Ubisoft; we were looking at them going, ‘Rayman's good’, and they were going, ‘Banjo is good.’ We always kept an eye on Rayman because he's a super cool character. He's Ubisoft's mascot, right,” he says. “He's a fantastic character. I feel like as a Brit, we like that kind of fart humor. I feel like it's built into British culture, right? So to get to work on that kind of character is great for British people because it's right up our street.”

While many games Kirkhope has composed for have featured spooky levels - think Banjo-Kazooie’s Mad Monster Mansion and Donkey Kong 64’s Creepy Castle - Land of the Livid Dead required the musician to take things up a notch.

“When I first started on it, I was expecting it to be that kind of traditional Rayman, quirky, irreverent, farts and all that stuff that goes on in Rayman, which is really cool. Right up my street, that is. But it wasn't. I got given the Land of the Livid Dead, which is really dark,” he says. “When I submitted ideas, they kept saying, ‘no darker, no darker, darker.’ I had to write quite dark music. They described it as ‘Rayman's no longer a kid. He's more of a teenager now. He's kind of a bit more mature.’ So I was like, ‘Oh.’ It was good fun to do. Because I like writing kind of spooky music with a bit of a quirk in it. I guess a bit Danny Elfman-y, probably.”

With the soundtrack being so dark, I began to worry it would lose some of Kirkhope’s trademark whimsy. I needn’t.

“I feel like it's got darkness in it, but there's still a little bit of humor. Because I think you can't do Rayman with that little bit of a nod and a wink, you know. And I feel like it's always got a bit of that. So it is dark. But it's still got a little bit of something in it just to maybe put a smile on your face, or maybe not scare you too much,” he grins.

I could be there. I could do that. Get me my bagpipes.

Over the last few years, Kirkhope has built a strong relationship with Ubisoft. His work on the Mario + Rabbids series was fantastic, and clearly it was good enough for them to call him back. I had to ask if Rayman was the only property he was working on, or whether, maybe, just maybe, this was a precursor to the troubled Beyond Good and Evil 2.

“No,” he says, chuckling. “It looks absolutely giant. I've got to say, when they showed it at E3, I thought it looked super cool. I'd work on anything for Ubisoft. I really would, because I've always had a good time with them.”

I decided to put that to the test. Would Grant Kirkhope work on an Assassin’s Creed game, and if so, when in the world would he choose?

“I'd love to have a crack at that, I've got to say. That would be a really cool project to work on, because it's such a cool game. And it's been through so many iterations of different scenarios,” he says. “London, Japan, the big massive Viking one. I’d love to do it. I think it’s unlikely, but I’d love to.

“Since I’m Scottish, it would have to be the whole Scotland-against-England thing. Back in the Middle Ages. William Wallace. Robert the Bruce. I could be there. I could do that. Get me my bagpipes.”

My chat with Kirkhope was brief. I only had 15 minutes with him, and despite Ubisoft having representatives on our call, I had to ask about some of his other work in quickfire fashion while I had the chance.

Firstly, I needed to know whether he thought the DK Rap, a song he created and has so often called “the worst piece of rap music ever,” would fly today.

“I feel like it's definitely a moment in time. I think at the time, the little kids got the joke, but the kind of cool teenagers are like, ‘this is the worst rap track in the history of rap tracks,’ and that’s because it is,” he says. “I feel like it's in the bracket of being so dreadfully bad that people love it and have a laugh with it. I feel like it's worked out in the end. I've had to wait, like, 25 years, but it got there in the end.”

What about the Goldeneye pause music? A viral TikTok video brought that back to the spotlight in the last few years.

“I claimed my TikTok artist profile. That song has 115 million plays. I have to watch that video every time it comes on my feed because he did such a good job. It’s fantastic, isn’t it?” Kirkhope says, smiling.

“I feel like I write these great, complex, orchestral pieces of music, and I'm proud of myself. And everyone's like, ‘yeah, but the DK rap, man, you know?’ Honestly, there are plenty of composers that never get known for anything. So if I'm known for the DK rap and the Golden Eye watch pause music, I'll take it any day. I feel like being known for anything is super humbling and just amazing that anybody gives a shit, right? So, yeah, I'll take it any day.”

Finally, with Donkey Kong rumored to be next in line for the Nintendo movie treatment, I had to ask Kirkhope how much he’d want to compose for it.

“Oh man, absolutely. Right now, this second, I’d drop everything to do that,” he says. “Even better, I’d do it with Dave [David Wise, composer on Donkey Kong Country, Donkey Kong Land, and Super Smash Bros. Melee, among others],” he tells me. “You can't do DK without Dave Wise's da-da-da-da, da-da-da-da, that tune [Donkey Kong Country’s Jungle Groove], and the DK rap. You've got to have those two.”

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

This Unexpected Antique Is Making a Major Comeback—and It Might Be Hiding in Your Grandma’s Art Collection

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This centuries-old style brings fresh character to the walls.

Vintage art adds soul to a space. There’s just something about that story and patina that lends itself to more personality for your walls. While oil paintings and tiny art have certainly had their time to shine, there’s another antique art form that’s taking center stage: They’re called reverse glass paintings.

Popular in India in the 18th and 19th centuries, reverse glass paintings are illustrations that have been hand-painted onto the back of glass, giving them a painterly, colorful look. Annalise Holasz, founder of vintage art gallery Hue + Holasz, says that these works have a “really naïve” quality since each is painted by hand, and the “little variations make each one special.”

Holasz also notes that their frames, often made from reclaimed wood, are particularly special, since they provide a charming, aged, and even battered appearance. These artworks can showcase visuals like flowers, plants, Indian princes, boats, and shells, as Holasz says, “but animals are probably the most common,” and include birds, fish, sea creatures, and jungle animals.

To learn more about reverse glass paintings, we tapped the experts, who are sharing what to look for when starting your own collection.

Alexander Widener, owner of Widener Company, believes that reverse glass paintings are rising in popularity because “they capture the spirit of craft and yesteryear that so many of us are craving in our increasingly digital world.” It also doesn’t hurt that earlier this year, designer Tory Burch gave Vogue a tour of her Paris apartment, where reverse glass paintings were spotlighted in her powder room.

“I think people are drawn to how fun and playful they are,” Holasz says. “There’s often a subject that feels personal, and the combination of vibrant color and a characterful wooden frame appeals.”

Melanie Lissack of Melanie Lissack Interiors believes that reverse glass paintings are trending because they are eclectic, unique, and vintage in style while still having a modern twist.

While current-day pieces can look remarkably similar to the bygone versions, Lissack thinks they just don’t carry the same charm as the antique pieces—as striking as they are. Those in the know don’t look at the artwork first; they look at the frame. For instance, Lissack is on the lookout for slightly worn frames.

Widener points out that if you’re questing after particularly old reverse glass paintings, you should look for bubbles in the glass, a sign that the glass was handblown. This was a standard practice up until the early 20th century.

Also, it can be as simple as buying the motifs you love. For Widener, it’s country scenes and images of animals. He’s also not put off if they don’t have a frame at all.

“If you aren’t heading to Jaipur anytime soon, then Etsy and eBay are great places to look,” Holasz shares. “Sellers restock weekly. I’d browse quite a few listings before choosing, because the frame can make such a difference. They can sell pretty quicky as they are always in demand though, so it’s worth doing some research to shortlist styles you like so you can move quickly when the right thing comes along.”

If you’re searching for the truly antique varieties (made in the 18th and 19th centuries), Holasz adds that these are likely more available at auctions.

Also, don’t count out flea markets and antique shops for these finds. For reverse glass paintings, according to current eBay listings, you can expect to pay as low as $5 all the way up to $5,000. The prices for these paintings tend to be a steal these days since they’re just starting to have a moment, so snatch them up before the prices rise.

While you can certainly give the spotlight to a single reverse glass painting, many designers believe these diminutive pieces look best when hung together in small groups.

As for rooms where these works look their best, Holasz thinks they look “really adorable” in children’s rooms, from nurseries to bedrooms to playrooms. They also act as hardy artwork that can be displayed in kitchens and baths, since, as Lissack says, the paintings are “protected by being on the rear side of the glass.”

Also, don’t be afraid to go a bit rogue with these pieces. For example, Holasz likes to mix them into a gallery wall alongside oil paintings, drawings, and sketches, since the different surfaces create visual interest. And you don’t have to hang them on the wall—they also look great leaned on bookshelves.

Widener, who often scores unframed versions, prefers to display them on wire plate hangers.

“When placed in a window, the light comes through in a really beautiful way,” he says.

Google is entirely disabling Search in schools that don&#8217;t want AI

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Students in schools that reject Google’s AI will see notices that Search isn’t available.

Students in schools that reject Google’s AI will see notices that Search isn’t available.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Google is completely turning off Search in schools that reject its AI features, the company told The New York Times. The decision was also shared in a broader education-related update from Google on Tuesday, saying that access to Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom, and Google Search “will be entirely off” for K-12 student accounts unless “administrators explicitly turn them on.”

Administrators will not be given the option to toggle Gemini-powered features off without disabling Search, however. Chris Phillips, Google’s vice president of education, told the NYT, “Search and AI are deeply integrated,” adding that schools that disable Google’s AI features “will not have access to search, and they’ll see a message from their school that indicates it’s not available.” Google didn’t immediately respond to The Verge’s request for comment.

The announcement comes just months after Google turned its Gemini AI assistant on by default inside the Google Classroom app, which is used by more than 150 million teachers and students globally. The move added to skepticism surrounding Google’s introduction of AI in schools amid reports of declining test scores and other impacts on students’ learning abilities. Last month, lawmakers pressed Google on the “potential harms” of putting its AI products in schools. Though Google is no longer turning on AI features in schools by default, this choice comes at a cost.

The company is also attempting to address concerns about students using its AI features to find “shortcuts” instead of solving problems themselves. Google is embedding its Guided Learning feature, which is supposed to help students work through problems, into its AI Overviews in Search. Google will begin testing this feature in select US schools this month.

Hubble Telescope sees cosmic 'cat's tail' that could wrap around Earth and the sun 500 times

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Latest space missions, discoveries and exploration updates.

Stargazing tips, cosmic events and expert insights on the universe

Start exploring exclusive deals, expert advice and more

Space news, cosmic updates and expert insights straight to your inbox.

Breaking space news, the latest updates on rocket launches, skywatching events and more!

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Breaking space news, the latest updates on rocket launches, skywatching events and more!

Sign up to our monthly entertainment newsletter to keep up with all our coverage of the latest sci-fi and space movies, tv shows, games and books.

Discover this week's must-see night sky events, moon phases, and stunning astrophotos. Sign up for our skywatching newsletter and explore the universe with us!

Space.com's Sci-Fi Reader's Club. Read a sci-fi short story every month and join a virtual community of fellow science fiction fans!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

"It's a testament to how much these massive planets really influence their surrounding dust and icy bodies out to hundreds of astronomical units."

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Using the Hubble Space Telescope, astronomers have investigated a cosmic cat's tail, finding it likely formed after two Pluto-sized bodies smashed together.

The feature, nicknamed the Cat's Tail, extends out from the debris disk known as Beta Pictoris which is located in a planetary system located 63 light-years away. This tail would put that of the average tabby to shame: It's so long that it could curl around the sun-Earth system about 500 times. That's about as long as 200 trillion house cats.

The major difference between our solar system and the planetary system that hosts the Cat's Tail is the latter's relative youth. While our solar system is around 4.6 billion years old, Beta Pictoris is a cosmic kitten no more than a few million years old. That means it is still host to collisions between planetary bodies similar to the ones that rocked the solar system during its infancy.

The team behind this research gained new insights into the Cat's Tail using 14 years of observations. This allowed them to solve the long-standing mystery of the violent conditions under which it formed.

"The Cat's Tail is thought to be the aftermath of a collision between large bodies — think something like Pluto," team leader Arin Avsar of the University of Arizona said in a statement. "Oddly, this feature had previously only been seen with the James Webb Space Telescope (JWST), which detects infrared light, but remained invisible at shorter wavelengths, which has puzzled the community for a while."

Comparing Beta Pictoris and our solar system, one thing rapidly becomes apparent: our cosmic neighborhood is relatively well-ordered. The planets orbit the sun with most space rocks neatly delegated into bands: the main asteroid belt between Mars and Jupiter, and the Kuiper belt out beyond the orbit of Neptune.

However, this is a stark contrast to the solar system's violent and chaotic youth. Back then, the solar system was host to an infant star surrounded by a swirling disk of gas and dust in which collisions between bodies called planetesimals — the building blocks of planets — were commonplace.

Astronomers can't look back to the solar system's infancy, so to learn more about how our cosmic backyard took shape, they study nearby young planetary systems. Systems like Beta Pictoris. That doesn't mean it has been quick to divulge its secrets, though.

"We think the Cat's Tail has eluded our observations in visible light until now because of what it's made of," Avsar explained. "It consists almost entirely of what we call organic refractory material, which is heavily carbon-based. These long chains of carbon atoms absorb visible light, so they're quite black."

This new study revealed that the feline feature is cat-like in more ways than just its shape. It's also pleasingly fluffy, Hubble observations suggest. That's because the grains that compose it are extremely porous.

"How you can get dust grains that small that are as porous as we see here no one really understands, and that's part of why we study this feature," Avsar said.

"It's possible that the velocities involved in the collisions were so high that the carbon grains vaporized, turned into gas, and then recondensed as they cooled, and that's how they became so porous," team member Kevin Wagner of the U of A said.

The scientists also discovered a hitherto undiscovered feature in the Cat's Tail — a warp that seems to be the result of an interaction between the trail of material and one of the planets in the system, Beta Pictoris b, a gas giant planet 10 times more massive than Jupiter.

"This gives us another line of evidence into how giant planets influence comets through their immense gravitational force," Avsar said. "It's a testament to how much these massive planets really influence their surrounding dust and icy bodies out to hundreds of astronomical units [one astronomical unit being the distance between Earth and the sun]."

The team's research was published last month in The Astrophysical Journal Letters.

Robert Lea is a science journalist in the U.K. whose articles have been published in Physics World, New Scientist, Astronomy Magazine, All About Space, Newsweek and ZME Science. He also writes about science communication for Elsevier and the European Journal of Physics. Rob holds a bachelor of science degree in physics and astronomy from the U.K.’s Open University. Follow him on Twitter @sciencef1rst.

World of Warcraft's D&D crossover is neat, but inconsistent—novel on one page, then shrugging and pointing to the PHB you already own on the next

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

World of Warcraft's big crossover with Dungeons & Dragons is finally here—and despite being a huge flagship collaboration, one of the biggest MMOs meeting with one of the biggest TTRPGs, I'm sad to say I'm feeling just a touch whelmed. This sourcebook is inconsistent—filled with very cool character options in some places, and just pointing at the already-existing player's handbook in others.

That inconsistency doesn't let the whole project down, mind. There are still some rules tucked away in here that I think are great additions to any campaign, not just a World of Warcraft-themed one.

Let's start with the big one: Classes. WoW and D&D do have a lot of overlap in terms of class fantasy—there's not much in the way of appreciable differences between a Fighter and a Warrior, other than a few iconic abilities being missing from the pack. As a result, this rulebook essentially uses the pre-existing classes as a template to throw some WoW flavour on.

Some of these changes are meaningful—take, for example, the Death Knight. This uses the Paladin's chassis, but throws in a ton of impactful changes, replacing Lay on Hands with Death Coil, for example. It also throws a whole new raft of Death Knight-adjacent spells, adjusts your Find Steed with a cool undead ghost that can phase through objects, and more.

A lot of the classes are solved by custom spells like this—the Paladin, for instance, has just the one new subclass to cover for Holy and a bunch of iconic Paladin spells. But the balance of these is a touch skewiff in places. Take Divine Shield, for instance. This thing is straight busted.

It's a 4th level spell, which does admittedly mean you'll only get it at levels 13 plus, but it makes you immune to not only the damage of a triggering attack, but also all damage until the start of your next turn. AND you can't be moved or have your speed reduced. While absolutely correct for the flavour of WoW's iconic bubble, this is crazy overpowered.

And while some of these classes do have a lot new on offer, others are so thin on content it's a wonder why they're there at all. The rogue gets a particularly short end of the stick, for instance. Outlaw rogues are just given a reskinned version of 5e's Swashbuckler subclass (which does, in fairness, have some new Cunning Strike options to bring it up to parity). Otherwise, the book just points you in the direction of pre-existing subclasses.

Keep up to date with the most important stories and the best deals, as picked by the PC Gamer team.

Others are a complete miss, too. Shamans are all awkwardly stuffed into the chassis of the Barbarian, which only really works for Enhancement shamans—Elemental and Restoration shamans are pretty much just your standard D&D barbarian with some flavour-adjacent rage options, rather than the full spellcasters they are in the videogame.

This inconsistency in quality applies to character origins, too. Some of them have fully bespoke traits—Dracthyr get their visage forms, Void Elves get this cool void form that triggers when you hit identical numbers on an Advantage/Disadvantage roll, and so on. And then there are Orcs and Dwarves, some of WoW's most iconic races, where the book goes: Just use the stuff that's already in the PHB.

And yet, there's a lot to this supplement I actually like. When it's actually adding new options to the classes, I think they're cool—the Frost Mage flavoured feat, Fingers of Frost, gives you extra cold damage against restrained creatures, for example. But the real thing I would encourage you to just crib for your non-Azerothian games are its gathering and crafting systems.

In 5e (and the 2024 rules), crafting's always been sort of a joke. There are tool proficiencies, but they might come up every once in a blue moon. The actual Player's Handbook just says to half the cost of an item, then smacks you around the head with a massive downtime cost for anything particularly expensive—a suit of plate armor takes a whopping 150 days to craft (though you can split this with extra help). At which point you might as well just eat the GP cost and buy the damn thing.

This book, however, has some really neat rules for gathering and crafting professions. Having the right tool proficiency gives you access to a profession, which you can level up when your proficiency bonus increases to new tiers (which also increases the rarity of the items you can make).

Instead of making players shell out the gold cost, these gathering professions have a neat little table for getting enough golds' worth in raw materials to make the thing, with extra bonuses for beating the involved DC by five or more. When you get to higher levels, certain recipes might require items from specific regions—and you might only know how to make those items via recipes obtained from certain factions.

It's still all pretty lightweight (although each profession does have a useful list of suggested recipes it should know) but that's honestly a boon to this system, rather than a bane, because you could very easily just slot this whole mechanic into your homebrew campaign with very little trouble.

And, more to the point, you could very easily make entire adventure hooks out of going to get specific materials for a recipe here—which helps solve D&D crafting's main issue: It's not relevant unless you have literal months of in-game downtime to kill. While the book doesn't make changes to the time-to-craft issue, it's still a major step up.

The fact that there's plenty to like in this book makes it all the more frustrating that it's so inconsistent."

Renown's another mechanic I like. You get renown with certain factions based on your origins, but you can increase this renown through play to get certain perks. For instance, improved renown with Darnassus gives you the option to learn certain proficiencies, gain a blessing from its leaders, or gather extra materials with herbalism. My favorite of these is the gnomeregan Shield Guardian perk, which specifically states that if you dip below 50 renown again, the guardian turns on you. Sorry buddy, my creator says you dropped off.

Aside from these rules additions, there are also some solid dungeon adventures with their own bespoke stat blocks, extra magic items, and a respectable bestiary full of critters to toss into your campaign.

The fact that there's plenty to like in this book makes it all the more frustrating that it's so inconsistent—some areas have novel, if a little wonky, additions to the system. Others shrug like the book's designers simply ran out of time and budget, pointing towards resources that you already own.

However, if you are looking to run a WoW-specific campaign, or just want crafting rules that don't suck, it very well might be worth checking out on the strength of its secondary mechanics alone. Just don't go in there expecting a 1:1 replication of the MMORPG. This is still Dungeons & Dragons—and in some places, I mean that literally.

Harvey's history with games started when he first begged his parents for a World of Warcraft subscription aged 12, though he's since been cursed with Final Fantasy 14-brain and a huge crush on G'raha Tia. He made his start as a freelancer, writing for websites like Techradar, The Escapist, Dicebreaker, The Gamer, Into the Spine—and of course, PC Gamer. He'll sink his teeth into anything that looks interesting, though he has a soft spot for RPGs, soulslikes, roguelikes, deckbuilders, MMOs, and weird indie titles. He also plays a shelf load of TTRPGs in his offline time. Don't ask him what his favourite system is, he has too many.

Emma Chamberlain Shares Her “Objects of Affection”

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

We’ve all seen just how glorious Emma Chamberlain’s L.A. home is—but have you ever wondered what the star’s most prized possessions are inside? If you have, we’ve got answers. In a new Vogue video, we dropped in on Chamberlain at home to explore what her personal “objects of affection” are. The short answer? One-of-a-kind items she simply cannot live without—from art to fashion and more.

Chamberlain began her house tour with a few of her fashion must-haves. She can’t imagine a day, for one, without using her everyday bags—including a Prada black shoulder schlepper or a green Comme des Garçons style. “I’m emotionally attached to these two bags—they’ve been everywhere with me,” she says. Then it was onto her most-worn shoes, including a white pair of the polarizing and fabulous footwear style, Margiela Tabis.

Next, the star took us to her gallery wall to share a few of her favorite art pieces, including a self-portrait. “I absolutely hate it, but I do like the eyes,” she says. There was also the abstract wooden sculpture-slash-painting of … a butt. “I got it at an antique store,” says Chamberlain. “It balances humor and aesthetic.” Also included in the mix were family heirlooms, like paintings passed down to her from her grandmother, or works made by her father. “There’s probably 20,” she says of her dad’s creations. “For the most part, he does a lot of landscapes.”

As for some of the more unexpected objects you can find in Chamberlain’s cabinet of curiosities? A big movie poster of Napoleon Dynamite (her favorite film), a drum kit—”I’ve been practicing and making some. . . improvements,” she says—and a wigged mannequin that she calls her body double. “I purchased her as a prop for my podcast set,” says Chamberlain. “She told me she wants to be called Emma.” There are two Emmas in the Chamberlain household.

Above, watch Chamberlain’s full “Objects of Affection” video.

AI Movie ‘Gods Don’t Give Gifts’ Gets Serious with AI-Generated Grimes Cameo, Times Square Marketing Push, Oscar Screenings

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

When we first wrote about the coming release of “Gods Don’t Give Gifts,” the first fully AI-generated feature film being self-distributed into movie theaters in the U.S., it would’ve been easy to write the whole thing off as a stunt.

Coming from YouTuber Gossip Goblin, who has an impressive and loyal following, “Gods Don’t Give Gifts” started earning comps from the press (and from the producers themselves) to the success Markiplier found when he self-released his film “Iron Lung” and made $50 million worldwide in the process. Would that success translate for an AI movie? A recent example from China suggests that may not be the case. Related Stories To Follow Her Best Picture Win, Siân Heder Opted to Make ‘the Hardest Story She Could Imagine’ How to Make IndieWire a Preferred Source on Google

It’s clear though the team behind “Gods Don’t Give Gifts” is treating this like a real project that needs to be seen in theaters and is worthy of serious critical consideration, and they’re doing that with real marketing dollars behind its theatrical push.

Today, Gossip Goblin announced that pop star Grimes is set to make a cameo in his film “Gods Don’t Give Gifts,” as is YouTuber Mike Majlak, and their involvement is being revealed via a massive Times Square ad campaign that is also launching today, all ahead of the film’s release next month on December 4. Gossip Goblin is even launching a contest for people to use AI to create their own short films within the universe of “Gods Don’t Give Gifts” that can net the winning creator $15,000.

“Gods Don’t Give Gifts” also sparked some headlines in the last few months with the news that the producers are plotting an Oscar campaign for Best Animated Feature. Turns out some FYC screenings have already started and a digital campaign begins at the end of November. All of the above suggests some real belief in the project, and with a collective 16 million views of the trailer thus far, they have reason to believe.

“We’ve been heartened already by the reaction,” producer Edward Saatchi of Fable Studios said in a statement. “One Oscar-Nominated animated film director compared ‘Gods Don’t Give Gifts’ after seeing it to ‘2001,’ and we’ve had great feedback from Pixar folks (part of Fable’s team is ex-Pixar and we hugely respect their work).”

Zack London, the man behind the moniker Gossip Goblin, in speaking with IndieWire ahead of the announcement, said he’s “fully cognizant” of the controversy around putting a movie made with AI into theaters, and he acknowledges that all of this is “a big, unproven hypothesis.” But he says it would be “disingenuous to write it all off” as just an AI movie or not a real project.

Getting screens is easy, but the real battle is making people believe there are actually humans behind this movie, even if the images are all generated by AI. Roughly 30 to 35 people are credited in the making of “Gods Don’t Give Gifts,” and a behind-the-scenes documentary that is also in the works is aimed to humanize the process of making the movie. It includes interviews with the film’s composer and voice actors and shows its Foley guy doing real post-production work. Even the act of getting a real person like Grimes in the film — whose image is generated to resemble her but who does real voice acting — required an elaborate process of photo mashing atop existing character design to fit her into the movie, as well as contacting the model makers themselves to remove built-in censors for generating images of a public figure’s likeness.

On one hand, “Gods Don’t Give Gifts” can be viewed as a scrappy indie made by a team of creatives, not just a sole prompter working alone, making a movie that wouldn’t exist otherwise. On the other hand, many would argue that the visuals on display would normally require a team of thousands to bring to life along with millions of dollars. Depending on where you fall on that spectrum may determine whether you also buy a ticket to see it.

But London and Saatchi have grander ambitions with “Gods Don’t Give Gifts” and AI filmmaking beyond just what it can do at the box office. Saatchi says that for as many great CG animated films have been released in the last 30 years since “Toy Story” first hit theaters, they’ve arguably all looked similar and been made with the same techniques. He views “Gods Don’t Give Gifts” as the next “Toy Story” moment in animation, with a movie that aims to look psychedelic and otherworldly, something that looks unique and looks like it could only be the product of AI generative models. Other films may use AI to create something cheaply that looks like a Pixar movie, but London wanted to make something that looked like nothing else.

“Pixar set the look for animated films in the ’90s — they didn’t use technology to more cheaply make a film that looked like ‘Beauty and the Beast,’ they created a radically new aesthetic with ‘Toy Story,'” Saatchi said. “That’s what ‘Gods Don’t Give Gifts’ represents: a wild new aesthetic for animated films that will define much of animated, actor-voiced films in the coming decades. This is that ‘Toy Story’ moment for our generation — a radical departure for animated films — you only need to see Grimes’ brilliant performance in the film to understand how much things are about to change for animated movies. And we hope the Academy will recognize that a film that is seminal (the first AI film), wildly technically novel and most importantly good deserves a nomination.”

Poll: Vote for Your PS5 Game of the Month (October 2026)

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ah, October, otherwise known as the month before GTA 6.

Indeed, it was an incredibly busy 31 days for new PS5 releases, following on from an equally manic September — and that's going to make this latest Game of the Month poll all the more intriguing.

October's most high profile title arguably launched just a few days ago: Phantom Blade Zero, the PS5 console exclusive that had been wowing onlookers for years with its gameplay trailers, finally arrived on the 29th.

The Chinese action game earned extremely impressive review scores, and it seems to have been a big hit with fans of the genre.

Meanwhile, Call of Duty: Modern Warfare 4 actually managed to deliver what many are calling the series' best campaign in years, and Castlevania: Belmont's Curse revived Konami's iconic franchise in some style.

Other standouts included the eye-watering Star Wars: Galactic Racer — a fantastic racer that's gone down far better than anyone thought it would — and Final Fantasy Resonance, which turned out to be a brilliant callback to the property's traditional JRPG roots.

Ace Combat 8: Wings of Theve deserves a shoutout too; another super highly rated release, and quite possibly the most engaging Ace Combat game of the series' modern era.

Then you've got the more divisive stuff that some of you are bound to vote for: both Stupid Never Dies and Hellraiser: Revival struggled to capture the hearts of critics, but they're the kind of games where if you enjoy them, you probably really enjoy them.

But as always, only one of these titles can be crowned Game of the Month. Before we get to the all-important vote, though, here's a quick recap of our 2026 winners so far...

Review: Zero Parades: Director's Cut (Switch 2) - ZA/UM Returns With Another Atmospheric, Cerebral Yet Overly Familiar RPG

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

How does a studio follow an era-defining masterpiece without the original creative forces behind said masterpiece? The answer, for better or worse, is to emulate the experience almost completely.

ZA/UM emerges from a controversy far too storied to cover here with a follow-up to existential crisis simulator Disco Elysium. Zero Parades is once again about a lone figure of questionable authority and skill, wandering a political melting pot of a city and trying to find salvation or oblivion. It’s achingly beautiful and constantly thought-provoking, but also very familiar for fans of Harry DuBois’ misadventures in Revachol.

The complex and multi-branching story follows Hershel Wilk — whose spy name is the distinctly cooler-sounding CASCADE — as she is reactivated after being put out in the cold following a botched mission. She awakens in her apartment with a hazy memory and her partner, Psuedopod, passed out in a chair. The meat of the story has CASCADE try to find out why her partner is in a comatose state and what exactly the Operant, her shadowy handlers, want her for.

Hershel/CASCADE ventures out into the bustling streets of Portofiro, an ideological morass that struggles and rebels under the watchful eyes of multiple factions. The societal clock has been reset to zero following the Latour-Woolgar Treaty and CASCADE’s handlers work intelligence for the ruling communist power of the Superbloc. Meanwhile, propaganda and word-of-mouth for techno-fascist group La Luz permeates every conversation she has.

ZA/UM doesn’t hold your hand in a narrative sense; the world around you is a complete mystery to the player and in most cases, the protagonist. You are left to glean an emerging plot from colourful NPCs around the city.

Not a traditional mainstream RPG experience by any stretch, the game plays out more like an investigative tabletop adventure. You navigate dialogue trees, while passive and active skill checks determine when and where information becomes available.

There’s a character build system of sorts that gives you relative freedom in what kind of amnesiac spy you wish to be. Yellow skills govern how personable and empathetic you are, pink focus on physical spycraft, and purple are technical aptitudes.

There are three status meters to balance throughout the game: fatigue, delirium, and anxiety. Actions and poor conversation choices have a direct impact on these levels, and maxing them out will force a downgrade of a corresponding colour of skills. These status effects can be managed by successful dialogue rolls, consumables, and several environmental interactions. Keeping one eye on these levels adds a layer of tension to the relatively action-free investigations.

Conditioning offers a further way to boost CASCADE's core skills. Throughout her wandering, our confused spy will discover certain thought patterns that she can condition, which in turn buffs a cluster of her abilities. These thoughts have specific unlock criteria that, much like everything else in the game, are never obvious. Unlocking them, however, adds an element of intrigue and abstract progression to your character build.

Another way to boost certain skills is by assembling an aesthetically ridiculous outfit from items picked across the city. Your skills are also vital in several decision-making encounters that occur throughout the story. These multiple-choice scenarios usually require a series of rolls and give your status levels a beating. Dramatic encounters are the closest the game gets to action and they contrast just enough with the rest of the gameplay to be exciting.

Navigating Portofiro is a smooth experience, falling somewhere between point-and-click-style pixel hunting and free exploration. ZA/UM has made incremental traversal and UI improvements over Disco Elysium that have really paid off. Movement and investigation are intuitive, and exploration is decidedly less laborious than in the previous game. There is still a lot of aimless wandering as you figure out the next objective, but a helpful map and scanning system keeps things moving at a healthy clip.

The city itself is dripping with atmosphere, thanks in no small part to the beautifully haunting score from Fernando Cabrero. The rogues and grotesques that our anti-hero interacts with are as meticulously designed as their dialogue, and the voice acting is superb (though CASCADE's inner monologue can get a bit jarring). It's one of those games where you can't wait to meet the next NPC and engage in a philosophical battle of wills and/or idiotic argument about what it means to be 'cool'.

Yet, as engrossing as this spy-out-in-the-cold odyssey is, returning fans will feel a pervading sense that they've been here before. Those that laud ZA/UM's debut opus may be clamouring for more political events set in a European dystopia, but they won't get much else besides that. Newcomers, on the other hand, are in for an engrossing, at times mind-bending treat and should certainly follow up with the superior predecessor once they're done here.

The Switch 2 version of Zero Parades is jam-packed with new content and native console features. Noteworthy is the inclusion of mouse controls, which work well and are much better suited to environment interaction than the regular controller setup. Alongside mouse controls, touchscreen support, and HD Rumble 2, this Director's Cut version of the game arrives with new outfits, a behind-the-scenes Backstage mode, and a reported 40,000 additional words in the script.

One downside is the 30fps target, which noticeably mars the experience of wandering the populated streets of the city on a large screen. The isometric perspective and UI-heavy gameplay feel much better to play undocked. It just feels baffling that 60fps was not possible, given the hugely demanding games the console can run at that level.

Going some way to balancing out the performance shortcoming is a generous suite of accessibility options, including colour balancing, contrast and dyslexia settings.

Fans who patiently awaited the next game from the Disco Elysium studio can rest easy. Zero Parades is another complex, intelligent and gorgeously presented RPG. It oozes style and boasts an engaging narrative and cast.

That said, it brings nothing new to the groundbreaking experience offered by its predecessor.

Ken Talbot has been playing games since they came in the form of cassettes. Over 30 years of experience in all genres and on all platforms, with a particular corner of his heart for Playstation and Nintendo. Ken has worked in the software industry for over a decade, which keeps him close to the tech and business decisions that drive the gaming industry. He loves to play everything, but is an enthusiast for survival horror and fantasy RPGs.
